Acknowledgments
I have principally to thank David Farrell Krell whose so-
journ at the University of Warwick as a Visiting Fellow was
the pretext for the occasion on which many of the early
versions of these papers were first read, whose loss to the
British philosophical scene is still deeply felt, and whose
friendship has given me more than a scholarly respect for
Heidegger. I must also thank the editor of Dvacritics for
allowing me to reprint John Sallis’s review of De l’esprit
(from Diacritics, Fall-Winter 1989), and John Sallis him-
self, not only for his fine contribution but, as editor of Re-
search in Phenomenology, for permission to publish a revised
version of David Krell’s “‘Spiriting Heidegger,”’ which first
appeared in Research in Phenomenology 18 (1988). Special
thanks go to Robert Bernasconi, Paul Davies, and John
McCumber for their helpful comments on the shape of the
manuscript and on individual papers. The volume is a bet-
ter one for their careful scrutiny. For occasioning this new
call on the timber reserves, Derrida himself cannot escape
a certain responsibility.

Responses and
Responsibilities:
An Introduction
n 1987 Jacques Derrida published a book entitled De l’esprit: Heideg-
ger et la Question.' An unusual title, though faintly familiar. And a
book whose public gestation some of us had witnessed. Unbelievably,
this 184-page text had been presented as a lecture in March 1987 at
the College International de Philosophie in Paris. It had begun in discus-
sion with friends at Yale about unanswered questions in Heidegger;
these were developed in outline form at a conference on Heidegger at
the University of Essex in May 1986.? And in response to the prompting
of Francoise Dastur, Derrida subsequently added as a long footnote
what is in effect a retraction of one the central theses (marked as such in
the subtitle): the unexamined privilege of the question in Heidegger.
But it would be a mistake to think of De l’esprit simply as a work
developed through discussion among friends. Although the longest of all
of Derrida’s readings of Heidegger, it has to be read in the light of a
longer series of texts, from “‘Ousia and Gramme,” ‘““The Ends of Man,”
and ‘‘Différance”’ (all presented in 1968), through to the “Geschlecht”’
series in the 1980s.° Derrida’s relation to Heidegger is, if anything, more
intimate than friendship. And the context in which it has developed has
been one of considerable hostility, both to Derrida, and to Heidegger.
Deconstruction had first been accused of being apolitical. But as Hei-


DAVID
WOOD
degger’s Nazi entanglements became a media event, the “complicity” of
deconstruction with the Heideggerian legacy was used as a basis for ac-
cusing Derrida of fascist tendencies. This is the logic of Habermas’s
treatment of Derrida in his The Philosophical Discourse of Modernity, first
published in 1985, in which he attributes to Derrida “‘the role of an au-
thentic disciple who has critically taken up the teaching of the master.””*
His chapter on Derrida immediately follows his chapter on Heidegger;
there is accusation even in the layout of the book.
In the same year (1987) as Derrida was presenting and publishing
Of Spirit, Viktor Farias’s Heidegger et le Nazisme was published, translated
into French from German and Spanish.° Although little of the material
in this book is new, the organization and presentation was new, and
owed much to journalistic skill. It also had many of the vices of journal-
ism—non sequiturs, establishing guilt by association, and so on. And, as
Philippe Lacoue-Labarthe comments, “‘his [lack of] philosophical un-
derstanding of Heidegger leaves one quite flabbergasted.’’® This book,
with all its faults, spawned a year or two’s bonanza in Heidegger pub-
lishing, in which it has to be said, serious philosophical and political
contributions were for the most part smothered by opportunist misrep-
resentation and self-congratulation on the part of those who had long
resented Heidegger’s appeal without being able to bring themselves to
read him.
Derrida wrote a properly dismissive review of Farias late in 1987,’
but what De l’esprit represents is a persistence in the philosophical task of
reading Heidegger that puts his detractors to shame. The only virtue of a
purely ideological reading of Heidegger is that its reliance on reductive
premises gives us a model of the destruction of philosophy that makes it
clear just how different Heidegger’s de-struction and Derrida’s decon-
struction are.
De l’esprit proves, if proof were ever needed, that it is possible to
do many things at the same time. The thematics of the intertwining of
questions is of course explicit in the book. Derrida responds to the
theme of the conference—Heidegger: Open Questions—with a list, a
list that is more than a list, more of a foursome, that he will knot to-
gether. But even before we delve into what the book deals with, what it is
doing is multifaceted.
1. He is answering the charge of being apolitical, or worse fascis-
tic, by attempting to locate the site of the political in and through Heideg-
ger’s texts.§
2. He is offering us an exemplary demonstration of how to read
Heidegger, of why we do not need to resort to book burning. There are


INTRODUCTION:
RESPONSES
AND
RESPONSHBILITIES
those who say we must now be more careful, more suspicious, more vigi-
lant when we read Heidegger. But without some sense of what reading
means, let alone reading Heidegger, these exhortations ring hollow. And
if what is called for is a reading that is not reductive, not one guided bya
priori principles, but one that exposes what has yet to be thought, or
what has not yet been completely thought through, we might think that
Derrida’s measured patient tone is preferable to the hysteria of those
who circle above the kill.
3. He is specifically tracing through Heidegger’s texts a term—
Geist, and its affiliates, geistig and geistlich—whose convoluted trajectory
serves to trace the path of Heidegger’s thinking more broadly.
4. He is continuing his interrogation of the importance of Trakl
for Heidegger begun in ‘‘Geschlecht II.”
Here perhaps we could mention an important point of origin for
De lesprit. There is much discussion of Hegel’s Geist in Glas,? but not of
Heidegger’s. In “Geschlecht II,” however, an essay devoted to the pur-
suit of the thematics and the traces of the hand in Heidegger, guided all
the time by the disseminating branching of Geschlecht (race, kinship, gen-
eration, sex, ““our community”’), Derrida had already cited Fichte’s non-
natural, indeed spiritual determination of what it is to be German. Der-
rida had touched on Heidegger’s (1945) explanation of his attitudes dur-
ing the Nazi period: that he had wanted to say yes to the national and the
social aspects of national socialism and no to its racial and biological
metaphysics. (This distinction Derrida will find marked as spiritual in De
Vesprit.) In this same essay, Derrida raises the question of technology,
and the absence of thinking, another one of the threads that will be taken
up in De l’esprit. Derrida addresses the question of animality through
Heidegger’s discourse on the hand. (‘‘Only a being who can speak, that
is, think, can have the hand.’’) We find the languages of “guiding
threads”’ (two in “‘Geschlecht II,” [the path of thinking as the work of the
hand, and the antipathy to the typewriter], four in De l’esprit) and the
same description of his method—first in terms of sewing, weaving, and
economy, and then—allow me to quote Derrida:
no word, no word for word will suffice to translate this word
that gathers in its idiomatic value stock, race, family, species,
genus/gender, generation, sex. Then, after saying the word
Geschlecht,
| amended or correct myself: the mark Geschlecht, I
clarified. For the theme of my analysis would come down to a
sort of composition or decomposition that affects, precisely,
the unity of this word. Perhaps it is no longer a word. Perhaps


DAVID
WOOD
one must begin by gaining access to it from its disarticulation
or its decomposition, in other words, its formation, its informa-
tion, its deformations or transformations, its translations, the
genealogy of its body.'°
There are possibilities of disarticulation specific to Geschlecht,
which Derrida is beginning to exploit at the end of this quote, but the
economic approach adumbrated here reappears, albeit transformed, in
De Vesprit. ‘“Geschlecht II’ unveils the tension between a polysemy, ulti-
mately controlled, that Heidegger affirms, and a dissemination that will
escape that control, which Derrida affirms—a tension between two
economies. De l’esprit, on the other hand, charts an economic movement
which begins (in Being and Time) with the containment/exclusion of the
problematic of Geist, by a mentioning-at-a-distance, and takes us through
to Trakl again, and Heidegger’s quest for a pre-Christian sense of spirit,
from the geistig to the geistliche. And when we reach Trakl, we find, of
course, again, Geschlecht, and we find it associated with Geist, gathered
under the common theme of Versammlung, or gathering: gathering into
Oneness. De l’esprit is, as David Krell writes, not part of the “‘Geschlecht”’
series. That series is itself broken and indeterminate; parts circulated
privately, perhaps others only rumored. But there are real points of con-
vergence, and a contiguity of concerns. As a final point of connection,
““Geschlecht II’’ ends with a list of ‘“‘five foci of questioning,’’ which over-
lap both in method and in content with the four threads of De l’esprit,
and with an abbreviated problematization of the site of Trakl’s Gedicht and
of Christianity, which returns again in De l’esprit. Perhaps we can expect
a ‘“Versammlung”’ series into which each of these will flow.
The essays collected here are a powerful set of responses to De
Uesprit, each distinctive, all valuable, and some highly original. This book
owes a great deal to the contributor of the first essay, David Krell. His
1988 review, substantially revised here, demonstrates an acquaintance
with Heidegger’s texts that at least matches Derrida’s. And instead of
Derrida’s final imaginary dialogue between “Heidegger and certain
Christian theologians,” Krell offers us a dialogue between Heidegger
and “‘the sister’’—perhaps Trakl’s sister—and broaches another more
geschlechtlich, and quite as disturbing silence in Heidegger’s reading. It
was David Krell’s visit to the University of Warwick in November 1990
that provided the occasion for the conference at which many of these
essays were first read.
Where Krell tries to push Derrida forward in a new direction after


5
INTRODUCTION:
RESPONSES
AND
RESPONSIBILITIES
a brilliant display of scholarship, Nick Land attempts the most extreme
outflanking of Derrida in his essay “Spirit and Teeth,” which might have
been subtitled ““Trakl’s Revenge.’’ Its basic premise is that neither Hei-
degger nor Derrida begin to grasp what Trakl really means. Derrida
reading Trak] is the unmarriage of scholarship with pestilence. Derrida is
not a werewolf—how could he expect to understand? Land’s strategy is
to demonstrate the depths of humanism totally untouched and un-
thought in Heidegger and Derrida alike: a plague on both their houses.
Trakl is a prophet of a libidinal economy, a radical animality that philoso-
phy cannot recognize and that thinking cannot think. Deconstruction
must give way to decomposition.
Gillian Rose’s essay ‘‘Of Derrida’s Spirit’? shares with Land a se-
verely critical response to Derrida (and to Heidegger), but from quite
the opposite point of view. Far from sharing Heidegger and Derrida’s
hesitations about spirit, Rose forcefully diagnoses their shared program
as a blind refusal of spirit’s diremption. If modernity is constituted by
“the unbearable
.
.
. diremption between subject and substance, be-
tween the forms of legal equality and the actuality of systematic inequal-
ity,’’ neither Heidegger nor Derrida begin to address this. Instead of
pursuing the political history of law and its inversions in the light of Kant
and Hegel, they treat spirit as mere “‘thematics,” as abstract universality.
And instead of recognizing (let alone trying to resolve) our predica-
ment—that we always find ourselves in a middle defined by the tension
between “‘the freedom and unfreedom of modern law’”’—Heidegger re-
gresses to prelegal forms of force: power, violence, evil, etc. Heidegger’s
musing on Trakl and the gevstliche “‘marks a further retreat from an un-
bearable political and social actuality that he still would and could not
think—and which Derrida, after him, will not and cannot reconstruct.”’
In a long supplementary note, Rose critically assesses Derrida’s com-
mentaries on texts by Cohen and Benjamin in his “‘Force of Law”’ essay,"
concluding that he offers us a false and philosophically and politically
stultifying opposition between primeval violence and formal law.
David Wood pursues the question of the misrecognition and mis-
reading of Heidegger through a comparison between Adorno’s inaugu-
ral lecture of 1931 (‘The Actuality of Philosophy’’)—which among
other things repudiates the language of spirit—and the economics of
Heidegger’s “‘avoidance”’ of Geist from 1927 until his lectures on Hegel’s
Phenomenology of Spirit in 1930-31. Heidegger’s Rectoral Address is, he
argues, an exemplary, if tragic case of the attempted actualization of
philosophy. Wood contests Derrida’s account of Heidegger’s treatment
of Geist, claiming that it is groomed for subsequent deployment by the


DAVID
WOOD
treatment it is given at the end of Being and Time. What neither Heideg-
ger nor Derrida notice is that the deployment of Gezst in the Rectoral
Address is the deployment of a logic of vertical integration, of leadership
and obedience, of participation. Heidegger recycles the structures of de-
pendence he himself diagnosed in medieval ontology as a logic of spiri-
tual regimentation.
Geoffrey Bennington’s essay “Spirit’s Spirit Spirits Spirit” con-
cerns itself with the method Derrida is employing in De l’esprit. He distin-
guishes two models of deconstructive reading. The first tries to
disentangle the quasitranscendental elements of a text, and show how
they bring about a “complex unity of possibility and impossibility” (e.g.,
Derrida’s readings of Rousseau, Plato, Kant, and Hegel); these readings
could be called affirmative, celebratory. The second reads a metaphysical
line against the overt antimetaphysical line of the text (e.g., Lacan, Fou-
cault, Searle) and is in effect critical. If we accept that a confident assign-
ment of authorial responsibility, or “‘signing,’’ marks out the second
critical reading, where, asks Bennington does this leave De l’esprit? Ben-
nington argues that while Lyotard’s judgment on De l’esprit would assign
it to the second category, Derrida’s long footnote compromising the
privilege of the question, and Heidegger’s unsettling attempts to think
the geistliche in Trakl, together disturb the very opposition between these
two types of reading.
Simon Critchley’s essay ““The Question of the Question: An
Ethico-Political Response to a Note in Derrida’s De l’esprit’”” takes up
even more centrally the problem posed by the famous seven-page foot-
note,'? although without Bennington’s sense of there being distinct al-
ternative strategies. Indeed, he describes De l’esprit as a classic example
of deconstruction: “‘the strictest and most rigorous determination of fig-
ures of oscillation or undecidability in a text.”” Derrida deals in this book
with the reductive critique of Heidegger’s politics, while responding at
the same time to the claimed poverty of politicals in his own work. Ac-
cording to Critchley, Derrida’s response is one that opens Levinasian
horizons. By asking “the question of the question’’ Derrida challenges
the priority of ontology and finds in language an irreducible dimension
of responsibility. Understood in this way, deconstruction is an ethical
demand in the Levinasian sense. Critchley concludes however with a se-
ries of questions the import of which is to suggest that when Derrida
quotes Kierkegaard’s ‘““The instant of the decision is madness” he is ex-
posing the paucity of deconstruction’s capacity to illuminate political
thought. Is there (not) a refusal of politics in Derrida’s work?
In “Spirit’s Living Hand’”’ Will McNeill initiates another conver-


INTRODUCTION:
RESPONSES
AND
RESPONSIBILITIES
sation, this time a response, in effect, by Heidegger both to Derrida’s De
Uesprit and to his treatment of the hand in ‘‘Geschlecht II.” McNeill is
pursuing in an affirmative way what precedes questioning for Heidegger,
and a couple of (Heideggerian?) ghosts in Derrida’s own text. There is
the question of what draws Derrida into his own treatise. And there is the
recurrent thematics of sheltering. Is Derrida’s De l’esprit, which seeks the
rule of ““Heidegger’s spiritual idiom”’ not itself perhaps a work of spirit?
The long footnote (see above) that Derrida appends to De l’esprit is an
attempt to deal with the problem posed by Heidegger’s reference to a
Zusage (promise, pledge) of language prior to questioning, which would
seem to torpedo his claim that Heidegger never questions the privilege
of the question.'* McNeill takes us back, instead, to Heidegger’s History
of the Concept of Time: Prolegomena (1925) and shows us how that privilege
is already in question before Being and Time. The account Heidegger
gives there of the hand, of orientation, of a primordial touching and
sheltering will shadow the exposition of Being and Time, a new line of
ghost that will not be exorcised, even if it withdraws. Derrida is simply
wrong to say that Heidegger only deals with the hand in the singular. But
more radically, McNeill is attributing to deconstruction the very carica-
tural gesturing that Derrida ascribes to Heidegger’s reading of Trakl.
Derrida’s focus on the privilege of the question in Heidegger is under-
mined not only by his own long footnote, but also by the detailed course
of Heidegger’s early writing. Is there not a relentlessness to deconstruc-
tive reading that can mask the “fragility of the word,” the living hand
that writes?
Each of the papers by McNeill, Bennington, and Critchley can be
seen to be beginning to register the consequence, anticipated by John
Sallis in his remarkable essay “Flight of Spirit,” that Derrida’s footnote
must bring him trouble. For Derrida is all too successful in showing
“‘how various, seemingly isolated, threads in Heidegger’s texts are knot-
ted together.” A disturbance of one will disturb all. Moreover it is Sallis’s
contention that ‘‘the retraction of the privilege of questioning” is an
event in Heidegger’s thinking, not just in Derrida’s book, and one that
effects a crucial interruption of metaphysics: ‘“The privilege of the ques-
tion is knotted together with the determination of essence as ground.
One cannot retract the privilege of the question without also disturbing
the metaphysical determination of essence. Without also, of course, dis-
turbing metaphysics as such, its essential determination, the as such ‘as
such.’ ” Sallis imagines a double of De l’esprit returning to haunt it, one
that takes to heart the lesson of the footnote, one that would take our
reading of Heidegger yet further.


DAVID
wooo
Derrida’s Of Spirit is a response to a response to a response, a
highly selective attempt to think philosophically the entanglement of Hei-
degger’s thought with Nazism, not in order to assess Heidegger’s formal
responsibility for his blindness or subsequent silence, but to allow us to
begin to undertake, through Heidegger’s thinking, and for all its faults, a
meditation on response-ability. Derrida’s response to Heidegger is an at-
tempt to continue to think responsibly in the face of the kangaroo courts
and lynch mobs of modern philosophical (and not so philosophical) jour-
nalism.
Derrida has suggested that deconstruction can be understood as
[a] sense of responsibility without limits, and so necessarily ex-
cessive, incalculable, before memory.
.
.
. a responsibility be-
fore the very concept of responsibility that regulates the justice
and appropriateness of our behaviour, of our behaviour, of
our theoretical, practical, ethico-political decisions.'*
The papers in this volume are united in their concern with responses and
responsibilities. Beyond the essential scholarly requirement, elsewhere
curiously often withheld, to read a text attentively (and to attend to the
stratified situation to which the text itself responds), on which Derrida
has rightly insisted in response to the many misunderstandings and spe-
cies of impatience to which his work has given rise, the theme of respon-
sibility powerfully opens up the ethical dimension of deconstruction,
allowing us to see why deconstruction has always been ethics and needs
no supplement in this respect. This is far from making responsibility (or
deconstruction) univocal or unproblematic, and these papers exploit its
multidimensional character. We have a response that takes up and takes
forward into extraordinary territory Derrida’s invocation of “the path of
the altogether other” (Krell); a reassertion of Derrida’s irresponsibility
both in providing “‘an apologia for Heidegger’s thinking in the face of
his Nazism”’ and in subsequently misreading the political import of Co-
hen’s and Benjamin’s writings (Rose); the interrogation of the responsi-
bility of Adorno’s critique of Heidegger in the light of Derrida (Wood);
an engagement with the limitations of Lyotard’s reading of Of Spirit and
a drawing out of the sense of Heidegger’s Geist in which Derrida’s read-
ing becomes a voyage of deconstruction’s reinscription (Bennington);
skeptical doubts about the limits of a politically responsible deconstruc-
tion (Critchley); the demonstration of how responsibility to the fragile
singularity of a writing is easily overshadowed by a powerful reading
(even one pledged to protect that very singularity) (McNeill); and the


INTRODUCTION:
RESPONSES
AND
RE SP OUN SB UL
TIES
redoubling reworking of Of Spirit to which a full response to Francoise
Dastur’s questioning of the privileging of the question in Heidegger
would give rise (Sallis). Derrida suggests that “‘any deconstruction of
[the] network of concepts [property, intentionality, will, conscience
.
.
. ]
may seem like a move toward irresponsibility.”!® He will undoubtedly
not have anticipated that form of (ir-)responsibility represented by Nick
Land’s corrosive nihilism, for which Husserl, Heidegger, and Derrida
are all wallowing in the humanist mire. That too finds a place in this
volume.
Many responses, many responsibilities. After Of Spirit, after the
long reply to Gerald Graff (‘““Afterword: Toward an Ethic of Discus-
sion’’),'® after his interview on Of Spirit with Le Nouvel Observateur,'’ after
his ‘‘Force of Law”’ paper, it should no longer be possible to misconstrue
the philosophical seriousness of Derrida’s response to Heidegger's
work, including the Rectoral Address. It should no longer be possible to
win plaudits by accusing deconstruction of political disengagement, or
of having nothing to say about ethics. Derrida has been as explicit as it is
possible to be about these matters. If philosophical and political debate
with deconstruction is to continue to deserve the name, it should begin
to respond to what Derrida is offering, rather than continuing to insist
that his table is bare. The papers in this book, even the most skeptical
and critical, would concur with this aim.


Spiriting
Heidegger
David Farrell Krell
Goethe says: “‘It is not always necessary for the true to be em-
bodied: enough if it hovers like a spirit [geist¢g] and produces
accord; enough if it wafts through the air, earnest and amica-
ble, like the sound of a bell.”’
—NMartin Heidegger, “Art and Space”
Will a more important book on Heidegger than Jacques Derrida’s De
l’esprit appear in our time?! No, not unless Derrida continues to think
and write in his spirit. Let there be no mistake: this is not merely a bril-
liant book on Heidegger, it is thinking in the grand style, wholly in the
spirit of Heidegger but also spiriting him across borders into strange
territories.
Spirit? And Heidegger? What is this apparent confusion of Hei-
degger with Hegel?
The book shows ten sections. It spans Heidegger’s Being and
Time, published in 1927,? and the Trakl essay of 1953, “Die Sprache im
Gedicht.’’? Its major way stations include the rectoral address of 1933
and the Introduction to Metaphysics of 1935.* Yet Derrida also manages to
draw Heidegger’s Nietzsche, Holderlin, and Schelling interpretations
into his account and to touch on scores of other themes and figures in
the Heidegerian text. I shall begin with a brief account of the book’s
gestation, then report on its ten sections, posing a number of questions
11


12
DAVID
FARRELL
K Bek ot
along the way, especially concerning the final turns the book takes in the
direction of Georg Trakl.
De l’esprit is not a member of the “‘Geschlecht”’ series, even though
that theme in Trakl’s poetry is the pole to which the book as a whole is
drawn. It is the text of a lecture delivered in March of 1987 at a collo-
quium organized by the College International de Philosophie under the
title ‘Heidegger: Open Questions.” A private seminar at Yale and a talk
at the University of Essex colloquium, “Reading Heidegger,’”® in the
Spring of 1986 were significant stages in the preparation of De l’esprit,
these in turn having arisen from work done in seminars during the past
several years at the Ecole des Hautes Etudes Pratiques on ‘‘Philosophical
Nationality and Nationalism.’’ No doubt the four principal ‘‘threads”’ of
De lesprit (A. The question of the question in Heidegger’s texts; B. The
“‘essence”’ of technology and the contamination of all essences; C. Ani-
mality and the philosophy of life; D. The problem of the ‘‘epochality”’ of
being) are visible in earlier works of Derrida’s, such as the various ‘‘Ge-
schlect’’ papers,® “‘Envoi,”’” “The Retrait of Metaphor,’’® and Spurs:
Nietzsche’s Styles.° These texts, culminating at least in part in the book
under discussion, complicate and deepen Derrida’s earlier work on Hei-
degger, best known through the articles, ““The Ends of Man” and “‘Ousia
and Gramme.’’° Yet in none of these earlier texts, nor even in the talks
immediately preceding De l’esprit, is there evidence of the spirit that is
haunting Derrida. For that one would have to turn to Glas, La carte pos-
tale, and the recently published Mémoires: For Paul de Man, Schibboleth:
For Paul Celan, Ulysse gramophone, Parages, and Feu la cendre.'! Yet I shall
here restrict myself to a brief introduction to Of Spirit, with its four
threads and ten sections.
1. “Spirit” is a word Heidegger advises the fundamental ontolo-
gist to avoid. It is one of those words like subject, person, rational animal,
consciousness, and ego that most need deconstruction. In Being and Time
he often places the word “spirit”? in quotation marks, as though men-
tioning it without using it; these “scare quotes” and other means of
avoiding a word (crossing through, erasing, employing the subjunctive
mood, and so on) preoccupy Derrida throughout his text. In choosing
the title De l’esprit, he is emphasizing another kind of avoidance—or at
all events another sort of lack or dissymmetry. The title is unmistakably
French, and not merely because the word esprit belongs to the French
language. The treatise form De... (cf. Traité de...
, Discours de.
. .)
conjures the seventeenth- and eighteenth-century French scholarly and
essayist traditions; De l’esprit is the title of a book by Helvétius burned
(the book, not the author, not quite) on the steps of the Palais de Justice


13
SPIRITING
HEIDEGGER
in 1759. The formula is ultimately Ciceronian, hence utterly Latinate, so
that the gap between spiritus and Geist—the entire problem of transla-
tion—opens at the center of the book. To be sure, the apparently aca-
demic and scholarly problem of translation is saturated with prejudices
involving nationalities and nations’ languages and cultures, principally
the Latin-Romance-Mediterranean as opposed to or opposed by the
Hyperborean-Nordic-Deutsch. De l’esprit is a political book through and
through, even though readers searching for easy answers to hasty ques-
tions are inevitably disappointed by it.
2. Derrida makes his contribution to the colloquium on ‘“‘open
questions” concerning Heidegger his own hesitations or points of indeci-
sion in reading Heidegger—‘“‘moments of gravest perplexity’ (DE, 21/
7). He asks how the four threads of hesitation (to repeat: the question,
essence, animality, and epochality) can be tied in a knot; he hypothesizes
that the word Gest, a word largely avoided in the Heideggerian text, may
serve as that node. For Geist is there wherever many are gathered (ver-
sammelt) by and into the unifying One. Not only for Hegel, but also for
Heidegger—-so that it is not a case of Derrida’s merely confusing these
two thinkers. Herewith the four threads:
A. Questioning is the piety of thinking, says Heidegger at the end
of ‘‘The Question Concerning Technology.”’!? In Being and Time he had
identified Dasein as the exemplary interrogating-interrogated being, so
that the questioning of being—and not some property of spirit—served
as the proper horizon for human being. It was not until very late in the
day (see ““The Essence of Language,” 1958)!* that Heidegger challenged
the supreme dignity of the question by asserting the preeminence of the
address and assent of and to language (Zuspruch, Zusage)—language
prior to all explicit questioning. (The long note dedicated to Frangoise
Dastur, in section 9 of the book, is essential in this respect, and we shall
have to pay special attention to it below.) Here is the site at which Der-
rida joins Heidegger’s efforts in thinking, reflecting on the promise that
the call of thinking and language enjoins. For Derrida, that promise is a
double affirmation, perhaps more in Blanchot’s than in Deleuze’s sense,
a “yes, yes” that somehow engages questioning. As the subtitle of De
l’esprit betrays, this first guiding thread is also the most persistent in Der-
rida’s text. ‘“Thus I shall try to show,” writes Derrida (DE, 25—26/10),
“that Geist is perhaps the name that Heidegger gives, beyond every other
name, to this unquestioned possibility of the question.”
B. The essence of technology is nothing technical. A recurrent
stratagem of Heidegger’s, one that would preserve the realm of essences
in the science of ontology as well as in poetizing thinking from the merely


DAVID
FARRELL
KRELL
contingent, the ontic, the prosaic. Similarly, at a certain point in his tra-
jectory, in the early 1930s, Heidegger is keen to rescue Geist from a cer-
tain destitution, disenfranchisement, and decrepitude, Entmachtung. He
will try to rescue it by force of an “‘essential will’’ or ‘‘will to essence,”’
Wesenwille. Derrida sees at work in the 1953 Trakl essay that same desire
to preserve spirit from “‘disessence”’ or decomposition, Verwesung.
C. The discourse on animality in which Heidegger participates
but which he by no means masters is perhaps the second most visible
thread in De l’esprit. How could it be otherwise, if “‘spirit’’ is traditionally
defined in opposition to nature, materiality, and bestiality; and if, as “On
the Essence and Concept of Physis’”’ indicates, Geist is aligned with
“grace,” the ‘‘supernatural,”’ ‘‘art,’”’ and “‘history’’?!4 However much the
animality of ‘‘mere life’? appears to be excluded from the purview of
fundamental ontology; however much life too appears to be a matter
that necessitates avoidance (precisely because life is neither Dasein nor
what is handy nor the at-hand); animality remains a problem that returns
again and again to haunt Heidegger’s thought, haunt it to the point
where the ontological difference itself is made to tremble. Even in later
texts such as What Calls for Thinking?! Heidegger’s discourse on man
and animality surrenders to a rhetoric so “‘peremptory and authoritar-
ian” that it appears to be “‘dissimulation in the face of an impasse” (DE,
28/11). That impasse becomes most visible in the 1929-1930 lectures
on “The Fundamental Concepts of Metaphysics,’’!® at the point where
Heidegger tries to compare the world-relations of stone, animal, and
Dasein. If the stone is worldless, and humanity world-shaping, animality
is both with and without world—animality is “poor in world.” Such
“world poverty’ is no doubt related to the animal’s lack of essential
“technique,” which is a lack of ‘‘know-how,” and its inability to pose
questions: thus the first three threads begin to twist and knot themselves,
as it were; and inasmuch as they have always done so in our history, as a
history of being, they point to the fourth and final thread.
D. The epochality of Heidegger’s history of being preserves
traces of a certain teleological thinking, especially in what it ‘‘forecloses”’
for thinking. Derrida sees such foreclosure at work in Heidegger’s inter-
pretation of the Platonic chora as a preparation of the Cartesian inter-
pretation of beings in space as extensio, and also in Heidegger’s (relative)
exclusion of recalcitrant figures from the history of being, figures such
as Spinoza, who cannot be readily subordinated to the epoch of subjec-
tivity. The telos of Derrida’s own book—f it is fair and accurate to apply
the same word to his endeavors here, as I believe it is—is to put in ques-
tion or to suspend Heidegger’s rejection of the entire Platonic-Christian


15
SPIRITING
HEIDEGGER
tradition in his effort to hear the word geistlich (but not geistig) in Trakl’s
poetry.
That Geist, geistig, geistlich form at and as the knot of these four
threads Derrida claims to know ‘‘ahead of time,” comme par avance (DE,
24/19). The word “hypothesis” is there merely out of courtesy. Yet if he
knows Geist to be the truth of being, the gathering of both Heidegger’s
and his own thought, the result of such knowledge is as fatal as it is para-
doxical. Derrida’s only surety is a “negative certitude,” and Geist re-
mains his “hypothesis”: the certitude of “not understanding very well
what ultimately regulates the spiritual idiom in Heidegger,” and the hy-
pothesis that an excess of clarity, “‘the ambiguous clarity of the flame,”
will take us to the knot formed by these four threads (DE, 29-30/12-13).
The catachresis of knot and flame will haunt us to the end.
3. Heidegger does not often try to define Geist. He employs the
word axiomatically. Yet whenever he does try to define it the results are
dramatic. In Being and Time it is, as we have said, a matter of avoiding
Geist. The word belongs to the ontotheological tradition and must un-
dergo Destruktion; it pertains particularly to the Cartesian res cogitans,
whose being—the being of the swm—has suffered neglect. Derrida
works carefully through section 10, ‘‘Delimiting Dasein from Anthropol-
ogy, Psychology, and Biology,” in which Heidegger emphasizes the com-
placency or ostensible needlessness (Bedurfnislosigkeit) that has
characterized the question of “‘the being of the subject, of the soul, of
consciousness, of spirit, of the person” (SZ, 46). Even when Heidegger
uses the word Gezst without quotation marks, he is mentioning it only in
order to enforce avoidance of it. Dasein, and not spirit, raises the ques-
tion of being—Dasein, and not some dogma of spirit, is to be the con-
crete starting point for fundamental ontology.
Here Derrida reverts to a suspicion he has entertained at least
since the 1968 ‘“‘Ends of Man” essay: ‘“This exemplarity can become, or
can remain, problematic.’’!’ The suspicion itself cannot readily become a
question, as Heidegger would wish, inasmuch as a kind of “reflection” of
questioning
is in play here. How question the structure of Fragen without
on the one hand confirming its power and on the other begging the
question? Precisely what one has always found most compelling about
Being and Time—the fact that the fundamental ontologist begins think-
ing about what he or she is doing and so engages phenomenology to
factical life—can also become terribly disquieting. How could one possi-
bly test the “legitimacy or axiomatic necessity” (DE, 37/18) of this point
of departure? A questioning of being-able-to-question: would not such a
reflexivity undermine the very order of existential analysis and all its


16
DAVID
FARRELL
KRELL
demonstrations, derivations, orderings, hierarchies, groundings, and
descriptions? Derrida is right to remind us that this issue of the proper
starting point, which Heidegger called den rechten Ansatz or angemessenen
Zugang, obsessed Heidegger before, during, and after the Marburg
years in which Being and Time had its genesis.
The principal issue elaborated by Derrida in this third section is
the problem of indifference (Gleichguiltigkeit, but also Indifferenz): the
indifference of the being that is at hand toward its own being, the
indifference (Indifferenz) that belongs to Dasein structurally and is essen-
tial to the modal structure of existential analysis, and indifference
(Bedtirfnislosigkeit) to the question of the meaning of being. It is indiffer-
ence in these three senses that causes Geist (along with consciousness,
subjectivity, person, etc.) to be interpreted as some thing at hand.
Existential-ontological analysis as a whole can be viewed as an effort to
deconstruct Geist in the direction of Gemuit (one’s “‘heart of hearts’’ or
innermost core) and Gemuit in the direction of Existenz: ““The apparently
new beginning of philosophizing [in Descartes] reveals itself to be the
sowing of a fatal prejudice, as a result of which succeeding ages ne-
glected a thematic ontological analysis of “Gemut’ along the guidelines of
the question of being and also as a critical confrontation with the ontol-
ogy inherited from antiquity”’ (SZ, 25); “‘the ‘substance’ of human being
is not spirit, as the synthesis of soul and body, but existence’”’ (SZ, 117).
Here one can readily see how the issues of essence and of the question
implicate the third thread, animality and the philosophy of life, as well as
the fourth, epochality. And it is also clear that the shift from Geist to
Gemuit, the latter being even less translatable than the former word, leads
us to suspect that the matter of “‘spirit”’ in Being and Time is, as it were, a
closed book. Spirit? And Heidegger?
4, Yet spirit returns. Returns to haunt. L’esprit revient. Naked,
transfigured, having shed its corporeal integument of quotation marks.
Although Heidegger begins by avoiding the word, it becomes clear that
“something of spirit can be subtracted from the Cartesian-Hegelian
metaphysics of subjectivity, something that points in the direction of
[faire signe vers| the Gemut”’ (DE, 44/23). This occurs not only after Being
and Time but also within it; for example, when Heidegger refers to the
“spiritual” nature of Dasein’s spatiality after having insisted that Dasein
is not a Geistding encapsulated in a Kérperding (SZ, 56). In section 70 he
writes: “Rather, because it is ‘spiritual’ [‘geistig,’ in quotation marks, to
be sure], and only for that reason, Dasein can be spatial in a way that re-
mains essentially impossible for an extended corporeal thing’ (SZ, 368).
Spirit also returns as the epitome of the vulgar understanding of


17
SPIRITING
HEIDEGGER
time—falling as one thing at hand into time as another such thing (SZ,
section 82). Quotation marks continue to constrain Geist here, yet the
privileged horizon of time indicates that the position of “spirit” and
“time” is not symmetrical with that of “spirituality” and “spatiality.”’
Heidegger writes, near the conclusion of section 82 (SZ, 436): “ ‘Spirit’
does not first of all [nicht erst] fall into time, but exists as the original
temporalizing of temporality.” “‘Geist” appears in quotation marks, as
Hegel’s word, and yet something can be translated from the latter’s su-
perlatively metaphysical text into Heidegger’s own analysis of Dasein’s
Zeitigung; something can be subtracted from the vulgar conception of a
plummeting spirit and so saved for an existential analysis of falling,
Verfallen. ** ‘Spirit’ does not fall into time; rather, factical existence ‘falls’
as lapsing [verfallende} from original, appropriate temporality.”’ The fall
is, as Derrida notes, “displaced,” becoming a lapsus from one mode of
temporalizing to another. All reverts to time—just as, much later, in
1953, both good and evil will revert to the flame of spirit (without “‘scare
quotes’’).
It is precisely here that a close reading of Heidegger’s recently
published 1921-1922 lecture course (Phenomenological Interpretations of
Anstotle: An Introduction to Phenomenological Research)'® would support
Derrida’s De lesprit. Factical life is animated as falling; its very Bewegtheit
or kinesis is what Heidegger calls Ruinanz. Mirrored in the world by a
kind of reflected light or Reluzenz, seduced by the carnival or masked ball
of what in Being and Time he will call inappropriate everyday existence
but here Larvanz, life plunges into the nothing, “the nothing of factical
life.”’ The mystery and miracle in such a Kierkegaardian-Lutheran-
Pauline fallen and falling world is that a counterthrust, Gegenruinanz,
can occur. It transpires in philosophy’s tendency to seek illumination,
Erhellung. Life becomes aware of its factical failings and lacks, its
Darbung, and struggles against the plunge. If Gezst remains virtually un-
named in such a scenario, the Erhellungstendenz of knowing and philoso-
phizing remains the only force by which total distraction, dispersion, and
diffusion (Zerstreuung) can be countered. Significantly, spirit does ap-
pear in this early lecture course when it is a question of “leadership” (das
Fuhrerproblem) of the university, of the “‘spiritual-intellectual prepara-
tion”’ of students, as opposed to the fatuous wit of mere “‘Geistigkeit,”’ in
quotation marks; or of the “‘spiritual condition we find ourselves in’’; or
of ‘‘the corruptors of spiritual life,” who abuse philosophy (Gesamtaus-
gabe, vol. 61, 70, 117-18).
5. De l’esprit now takes one of its most dramatic turns. The prob-
lem of quotation marks—one that has long preoccupied deconstruction,


18
DAVID
FARRELL
KRELL
which embraces “‘paleonymy”’ and inaugurates the “epochal regime of
scare-quotes’’—reaches a kind of crisis. For in the 1933 rectoral address,
“The Self-Assertion of the German University,” of which this section is a
close reading, Heidegger drops the scare quotes. Derrida pictures them
as pins holding a curtain that is lowered before a stage: as the pins fall
away, the curtain, which was always raised a crack, opens onto the play of
spirit itself. The raising of the curtain introduces a scene of academic
solemnity: Spirit itself, in procession, appears in mortar board and er-
mine in order to confirm the self-affirmation of and by the German uni-
versity. The prudence and methodological rigor of Being and Time bow
to the eloquent fervor of the newly ordained spiritus rector.
The spiritual mission of the German university under its new lead-
ership (Fuwhrung) sanctions the end of those constraints (“‘ ”’) that bind
Geist and geistig in Being and Time. Two decades after the Rektoratsrede, in
the Trakl essay of 1953, Heidegger will contrapose Trakl’s word geistlich
to the term gezstig, because the latter remains embroiled, according to
Heidegger, in the Platonic-Christian ontotheological tradition. How-
ever, in 1933 Heidegger sets geistig in italics, underlines and stresses it
rather than setting it off in constraining scare quotes; he ignores geistlich
(which of course would be odd outside the ecclesiastical context), and
does not suggest that gezst7g has anything to do with a tradition that has
to be deconstructed. Geistige Fuihrung will not be concerned preemi-
nently with such a Destruktion. At the core of the rectoral address Hei-
degger defines Geist, not in a Cartesian or Hegelian sense, but as
something that has to do with the mission (Auftrag) of the German uni-
versity as such. Derrida singles out four predicates contained in the defi-
nition of spirit which, in spite of the apparent distance of the rectoral
address from Being and Time, stand in discomfiting proximity to it: (1)
spirit is a questioning, understood as a will to knowledge and a will to
essence (Fragen, Wissenchaft, Wesenswille); (2) spirit expresses the spiri-
tual world of the people (geistige Welt, the latter being a crucial ‘‘cate-
gory” of Being and Time); (3) spirit is nourished by the forces of earth and
blood—even in the German university (erd- und bluthaften Krafte als
Macht); (4) spirit is resolve or resolute openedness (Entschlossenheit, one
of the most important structures of Being and Time, retained also, al-
though Derrida does not mention it, in Gelassenheit).}°
Derrida cites a long passage from the rectoral discourse, describ-
ing it as a celebration, exaltation, and kerygmatic proclamation of
spirit.*? It is a text of superlatives, of the essence, and of the utmost grav-
ity: Geist ist urspriinglich gestimmte, wissende Entschlossenheit zum Wesen des
Seins. “Spirit is originally attuned, cognizant openedness to the essence


19
SPIRITING
HEIDEGGER
of being.”’ Also of utmost violence and danger: Und die geistige Welt eines
Volkes .
.
. ist Macht der tiefsten Bewahrung seiner erd- und bluthaften Krafte
als Macht der innersten Erregung und weitesten Erschutterung seines Daseins.
“And the spiritual world of a people
.
.
. is the power of the most pro-
found preservation of its forces of earth and blood, as the power of the
most intensive heightening and most extensive shattering of its Dasein.”
Here Heidegger erects and exalts “the highest,” le plus haut. With these
words, le plus haut, one cannot help but think of le Trés-Haut in the
ethico-religious discourse of Levinas, and one will have to think of it
again in the closing pages of De l’esprit, where Derrida imagines a collo-
quy between Heidegger and a group of theologians. For the moment
Derrida indicates the highs and lows that often dominate Heidegger’s
discourse: one might think of that extended passage in his Nietzsche lec-
tures in which he comments on Zarathustra’s high mountains and deep
seas, the former having emerged from the latter and pointing back to the
depths.*' In the Rektoratsrede of 1933 it is a matter of the elevated his-
toric destiny and the hauteur of a people gathered in dieser hohen Schule
over the depths of earth-and-blood forces. Spirit here has nothing to do
with metaphysical subjectivity—at least in Heidegger’s own judgment.
“No contradiction with Being and Time in this respect,’’ remarks Derrida
(DE, 61/37). There remains of course the possibility that the new rec-
tor’s “‘massive voluntarism’’ remains embroiled precisely in Subjectitat
and that the union of Geist and Geschichte (geistig-geschichtliches Dasein,
geschichtlich-geistige Welt) cannot be disentangled from the Hegelian con-
text. Yet how disastrous such a context would be for both fundamental
ontology and Heidegger’s “other” thinking! For if Dasein and Welt are
unified in Geist; if the global phenomenon of being-in-the-world is spirit;
if openedness to the essential unfolding of being is Gezst; if the history of
being is the existence of spirit (Dasein des Geistes); then Heidegger’s
thinking is the mere epiphenomenal flurry of a certain right-wing Hege-
lianism, and, once again, the confusion will not have been Derrida’s.
Derrida does not dream of suggesting such a reduction. Not for
nothing has he been a kind of “envoy,” insisting that his French col-
leagues read and study Heidegger with the greatest care. For the grow-
ing number of philosophers in the English-speaking world who disdain
reading except when it comes to books and articles of bad journalism,
Derrida as envoy is no doubt tainted. There is absolutely nothing in De
l’esprit that the boulevard press in Paris and certain academic presses in
America and England will be able to tout as evidence of “Heidegger the
Nazi.” And yet the entire text is haunted by the ghostly return of a Geist
that always means more than either Heidegger or Derrida himself can


20
DAVID
FARRELL
KRELL
control. As though spirit were a kind of fungus, a mushroom thrusting
its head through pine needles on a forest floor.
Spirit and history unite for Heidegger in essential questioning. The
first and second threads of Derrida’s hesitation and perplexity return
again in the weft of ‘““The Self-Assertion of the German University.”’ Sci-
ence is das fragende Standhalten in the midst of beings as a whole (‘‘Self-
Assertion,”’ 12). Such science obligates the one who assumes leadership
(geistige Fuhrung) “‘to the essentiality and simplicity of questioning,” zur
Wesentlichkeit und Einfachheit des Fragens (‘‘Self-Assertion,” 17). Such
questioning understands itself as response—response to the call to re-
sponsibility. Once again, echoes of an ethico-religious discourse, echoes
that will return in section 9 when Derrida himself invokes the responsi-
bility of engagement. Thus Heidegger’s discourse of spirit would legiti-
mize National Socialism. One could turn against Heidegger the charge
he brings against Nietzsche in ‘““Who Is Nietzsche’s Zarathustra?’’ when
he writes of Nietzsche’s “‘supremely spiritualized spirit of revenge,”
hochst vergeistigter Geist der Rache.*? And yet one must also acknowledge
that in this very same gesture Heidegger takes his distance from National
Socialism, interrupts his commitment to what will prove to be anything
but a spiritualized politics of ‘‘earth and blood.”’ Ultimately one cannot
separate off spiritualism from biologism and naturalism, however, and it
is this contamination of the discourse of “freedom of the spirit” and the
“rights of man,” this complicity in which one is inevitably caught, that
haunts Derrida’s Of Spirit. No doubt one must choose the least hazard-
ous of such complicities and contaminations as best one can; to dream of
escape would be to give up the ghost. And so the other ghost, the ghost
of spirit, always returns, doubles itself, passes into its opposite, ventrilo-
quizes. ‘Metaphysics returns,’”’ writes Derrida, ‘‘and I understand this
as a returning-to-haunt; Geist is the most fatal figure of this haunting
return” (DE, 66/40). Even Heidegger, with all his scare quotes,
Anfuhrungszeichen and GanseftiBchen, cannot escape it: such equivocity is
of spirit, de l’esprit. It returns almost immediately, in the 1935 lectures
on metaphysics, and decades later, in the 1953 ‘‘Die Sprache im
Gedicht.”’
Spiritual Fuihrung is intro-duction into metaphysics, understood
as Hineinfuhren in das Fragen der Grundfrage. Heidegger’s geopolitical
analysis of spirit in 1935, an analysis conducted from the apparently sole
possible point of view of ‘the metaphysical nation” (EM, 29), oscillates
between the despair-beyond-pessimism of ‘“‘the darkening world” and
the hope-beyond-optimism of “new historical spiritual forces from the
middle.’ Derrida continues his analysis of this text, Introduction to Meta-


21
SPIRITING
HEIDEGGER
physics, in section 7 of De l’esprit. Having cited Heidegger’s ‘‘Weltpolitik
of spirit” and Weltverdiisterung of “spiritual decline,” Derrida now picks
up another thread, the third, which asks how the spiritual world is re-
lated to the world of beings that are ostensibly unlike Dasein. Animals,
for instance. Other animals.
6. Derrida treats at some length the analyses of animality and the
world-relation of animals as Heidegger develops them in his 1929-1930
lecture course, The Fundamental Concepts of Metaphysics: World—Finitude—
Solitude.** He emphasizes Heidegger’s hostility to Lebensphilosophie, em-
phasizes it perhaps excessively, inasmuch as Heidegger’s discomfiture
with transcendental phenomenology has much to do with his early con-
viction that philosophy must flourish on the basis of factical life and be at
one with it. This is not to say that Heidegger is a Lebenphilosoph, a life-
philosopher of the Bergsonian, Nietzschean, or Schelerian sort. But it
does mean that the polemic arises from a need and a frustration at the
heart of Heidegger’s own thinking. Derrida’s analysis fails to do justice
to the Diltheyan and Schelerian background to the question of life and
animality; it thus misses something of the centrality of this particular
thread—the thread of daimon life.**
A second difficulty with Derrida’s analysis is that it begins by an-
ticipating the “raising of the curtain” on Geist in 1933-1935. He pro-
jects the 1935 proclamation, “‘Welt ist geistige Welt. Das Tier hat keine
Welt, auch keine Umwelt,’’ back onto the 1929-1930 lectures.*° That the
animal has no world, or has-it-in-not-having-it, or is poor in world, would
amount to saying that the animal is poor in spirit. World-poverty would
be spiritual poverty. While I believe that Heidegger’s thought does be-
tray this tendency, and am therefore wholly in accord with the essential
thrust of the analysis, I believe it is essential not to confuse 1929 with
1935. The very power of Heidegger’s earlier lectures on animal life de-
rives from the fact that here he does not appeal to spirit. Here, as in Being
and Time, it is a matter of avoiding Gezst, even when it seems most appro-
priate to introduce it. As far as I can see, Heidegger does not even intro-
duce the word negatively, in scare quotes: he opposes the use of the
words Seele and Bewuftsein in his discussion of animality, but leaves Geist
altogether out of account. It is precisely Derrida who teaches us the im-
portance of observing scrupulously the use or nonuse of the geistigen
vocabulary, and such scrupulous care is called for here, especially here:
when at the end of his 1929-1930 course Heidegger tries to define the
human relation of world as Weltbildung, he engages in a long analysis of
speech as apophantic discourse, of the “‘as” that introduces human be-
ings to the realm of essences (the rock as rock, sunlight as sunlight, etc.);


22
DAVID
FARRELL
KREEL
yet he never appeals to the Geist that used to guarantee the traditional
access to that realm. It is precisely in the avoidance of the word that one
senses the hovering Hegelian spirit, not so much in the wings as sus-
pended in the machinery above center-stage. What Heidegger does in-
troduce are the following notions, each designed to specify the human
relation to the world and to exclude the animal from it: freedom, open-
ness to the world (Weltoffenheit) and the ruling sway of the world (Walten
der Welt), the difference (Unterschied) between being and beings, the con-
frontation or “hold” that is established between human beings and all
other beings (Entgegengehaltenheit), and the project and projection
(Entwurf) of being into beings that constitutes a human life. None of this
appeals overtly to Geist, to geistigen or geistlichen matters. If Weltoffenheit
characterizes humanity, Weltoffensein characterizes animality. Yet how
does -sein differ from -heit, indeed in such a way that Heidegger can be
assured that an “‘abyss”’ separates human beings from animals, as though
the world were constructed like one of those modern, cageless, animal-
friendly zoos?*° Precisely in this non-naming of Geist the spirit of meta-
physics (gen. subi. et obi.) returns. Heidegger tries to reduce the Offensein
of animality to its enclosure within a ring of disinhibitions, of passive
subjections and preprogrammed responses (Hingenommensein, Einge-
nommensein), a closed circle of benumbed behavior (Benommensein). Yet
the circle is, as we shall see, continually undone, the ring repeatedly shat-
tered. By something a bit like time, and something very much like death.
Derrida’s case is only made stronger if we insist on prolonging—
as Heidegger does—the avoidance of spirit. Heidegger’s effort to distin-
guish the world-relations of humanity and animality is fundamentally
aporetic: it is, as Derrida says, “‘nonplussed,”’ embarrassé. By reintroduc-
ing the notions of lack or privation, the very notions that he employed in
order to bracket the problem of “‘just-plain-life’”’ (Nur-noch-leben) in Be-
ing and Time (SZ, 50, passim), Heidegger reverts willy nilly to the mea-
sure of Dasein. Although Derrida does not state it as baldly as I do here,
I believe he would agree that the very effort to define the singular differ-
ence between humanity and animality inevitably obfuscates the ontological
difference. | would stress far more than he does the fact that Heidegger
uses the very same word to describe the world-relation of animals and the
appropriate comportment toward being that characterizes Dasein: if the
lizard sunning itself on a rock is benumbed (benommen), so is Dasein, not
only when it succumbs to the world’s distractions but also precisely when
it confronts the uncanniness of its existence in anxiety. Appropriate Da-
sein, rapt to the ownmost possibility of its existence, is an animal.2’
Here perhaps is where the ghost of metaphysics descends, not


SUE Sa
SPTRITING
HEIDEGGER
merely as a return of Hegelian spirit but as an epiphany of the daimon
that captivated Heidegger throughout his life. The nothing invades ani-
mal life and disrupts the ring. Death shatters that ring and signals the
way in which time—the marking of time—always bears a fundamental
relation to the animal’s life. The animal is thrown by way of its litter
(Wurf), cast into its life and projected toward its death as no stone is ever
thrown; the effects of that invasion, disruption, and shattering of the
ring by (something like) the nothing are felt also at the center of Dasein’s
hermeneutic circle. That circle and the ring of animality suddenly be-
come coextensive, if only for a fleeting instant. If Heidegger returns to
his bugs and his bees, apparently confident that the world of humankind
will prove to be essentially different, just as Hegel was confident that
spirit would assume essentially higher forms; if in other words the ‘‘trou-
bling affinities’ of Heidegger and Hegel on the question of animality
continue to plague us (indeed there are passages from the Beitrdge zur
Philosophie that seem to be lifted directly from the spirit and letter of
Hegel’s Encyclopedia); it remains the case that the daimonic, to daimonion
combines for Heidegger in mysterious ways the matters of godhead, life,
and the abyss of anxiety. Derrida more than anyone else would want us
to recall the note from the 1928 logic course that weaves a complex web
of knots with the themes of dissemination (Streuung), the overpowering
(das Ubermachtige), the holy and divine (Heiligkeit, das Gottliche), daimo-
nion, the nothing, and anxiety.?* Whereas Derrida stresses throughout
De lesprit that spirit is gathering into a unifying one, or Versammlung,
one must also acknowledge Heidegger’s insight during the period
1928-1930 that transcendence is dissemination into factical concretion,
embodiment, spatiality, and (something like) sexuality (Gesamtausgabe,
vol. 26, 173). Dissemination, and dispersion, Streuung/Zer-streuung are
also “‘of spirit.’’ Insofar as Geist is daimonic, it is no less disseminative
than it is gathering. It flickers like a flame. This qualification does not
cause De l’esprit to tremble, but it does perhaps suggest that the ‘‘Ge-
schlecht”’ project remains the more radical one. At the end of these re-
marks I will try to show why the latter project must continue. And it
ought to continue, not with an imagined dialogue between Heidegger
and the theologians—those diffident demonists—but with the already
initiated negotiations between sisters and brothers. For example, in
Trakl’s poetry.
Toward the end of section 6 Derrida’s threads twist together to
form several very tight knots. The animal (thread 3) lacks both the expe-
rience and the language of the as such, the language of essence (thread
2); lacking language, the lizard poses no questions about the rock across


2A
en a
ee
