SPARAT
sented in the form of a definition: § is P. And without any pos-
sible doubt, Heidegger takes it up for his own [la prend a son
compte]. He is no longer mentioning the discourse of the other.
No longer speaking of spirit as in Descartes, Hegel, or later
Schelling or Hélderlin. (DE, 58/35)
These confident assignments are complicated by at least two pas-
sages elsewhere in the book. The first of these comes at the end of chap-
ter 4, which has been plotting the later vicissitudes of the term Geist in
Sein und Zeit, after Heidegger’s early warning not to use the term, to
avoid it: Heidegger comes back to Geist in quotation marks, ‘“‘does not
take it up as his own”’ (prendre a son compte again, and this in spite of the
remark, a couple of pages earlier, that in these late usages from Sein und
Zeit Heidegger “‘finally takes up [prend enfin a son compte] the word
“spirit” as his own, but twice in quotation marks” [DE, 49/27], quoting
being a way of taking up without taking up, but of course the presence or
abs_nce of quotation marks would be a very crude criterion in general of
what it means to sign or take up as one’s own).® This inspires a ‘“‘provi-
sional”’ use, “‘for convenience,”’ of the use /mention distinction as a way
of describing this situation. First, says Derrida, Heidegger uses the word
spirit, but in a negative way, in fact rather mentioning it as a word whose
use should be avoided; then he really uses it ‘‘on his own account” [a son
compte, again], but in quotation marks, thus signaling a sort of quotation
or borrowing from the discourse of the other. But it is nonetheless this
second “‘use”’ which is also a “‘mention’’—and this quite complicated use
(or mention) of the use/mention distinction describes quite accurately, it
would seem, the sort of complicated imbrication of signatures that I was
suggesting marked out Derrida’s own “standard,” paleonymic reading
practice, on Rousseau, Plato, etc., with the play of crossings-through and
quotation marks that this implies, as indeed it is rapidly described once
more at the end of De l’esprit (DE, 177/108)—this second use-mention,
then, which is here presented as the first stage of ‘the slow work of reap-
propriation which will merge, as I should like to demonstrate, with a re-
Germanization” (DE, 44/23-24), the story of which Derrida goes on to
tell in the body of the book.
Let me not rehearse that story, but follow a little more this a son
compte, this ‘‘on one’s own account” or “for one’s own”’ that we’ve en-
countered so frequently in these pages. What happens when we finally
reach the moment, “25 years later,” in the text on Trakl, when Heideg-
ger approves Trakl for avoiding geistig and preferring gezstlich? We have
reached chapter 9 by now, and it looks at first as though these confident


88
GEOFFREY
BENNINGTON
decisions as to signature, as to whose account is to be credited or debited
around the use or mention of the word ‘‘spirit’’—it looks as though they
have to stop:
In this Gesprach, there will be no deciding [this translates on ne
décidera pas, which could also, perhaps more plausibly, be taken
as ‘‘we will not decide’’] whether the thinker speaks in his
name or in correspondence with Trakl. In the face of such
statements [i.e., statements saying, for example, “Spirit is
flame’’], there will be no deciding [or: we will not decide]
whether visible or invisible quotation marks, or even some still
more subtle marks, must suspend the assigning of a semple re-
sponsibility. In order to decide, a long meditation would be
necessary, before such an assigning, as to what Heidegger says
at the beginning about double speech and doubly addressed
speech—Gesprach and Zwiesprache—between thinker and poet.
It would be necessary to meditate on the difference but also
the reciprocity (Wechselbezug) between the Erérterung (the situa-
tion, the thought of the site, Ort) and the Erlduterung (the elu-
cidating reading, the ‘‘explication’’) of a Gedicht, the difference
between Gedicht and Dichtungen, etc.
But now, suddenly, something cutting through this apparently endless
complication of signing and quoting, using and mentioning, asserting
and reporting:
Just as I cannot translate these words without lengthy formali-
ties, so for lack of time I will have to restrict myself to this
gross affirmation which I think is hardly contestable: state-
ments like those I have just cited and translated by spirit-in-
flames are obviously statements of Heidegger. Not his own, pro-
ductions of the subject Martin Heidegger, but statements to
which he subscribes apparently without the slightest reluc-
tance.
.
.
. It would be completely irrelevant to reduce these
statements in ontological form to “‘commentaries.’’ Nothing is
more foreign to Heidegger than commentary in its ordinary
sense. (DE, 134—35/84-85)
So here, in a context where the uncertainty and indirection ap-
pears to be at its height, where the distribution of uses'‘and mentions
appears the hardest of all, where we are suspending even the decision as
to the suspension of responsibility, where we apparently cannot even


89
SPRATT S
Se PRUE, SPR
TS
SPIRIT
decide that the question ‘“‘who signs?” is undecidable, Derrida still does
not, apparently, hesitate to assign this assertion that spirit is flame to
Heidegger, not as something he invents, of course, but as something to
which he nevertheless subscribes, to which he puts his name. This is im-
mediately qualified further, however: of course, says Derrida, such state-
ments of Heidegger’s are inspired or induced by lines in Trakl, though of
course the lines Heidegger quotes are determined by what he is saying
himself—this coming and going being the very structure of the Gesprach.
But, says Derrida first, he’ll try at least “‘up to a certain point and provision-
ally” to sort out of this intermeshing what belongs to Heidegger, what
comes down to, or back to, Heidegger (ce qui revient a Heidegger, perhaps
what returns to haunt him), but then adds immediately that that’s really
not the question—I’m afraid I have to modify the translation at this point
as it is not strictly accurate; this is another reason why books one has
translated seem odd and return to haunt the translator:
Given this, the question that counts is not that of finding out
who says “‘spirit-in-flames’’—they both say it in their fashion—
but to recognize what Heidegger says of spirit in order to situate
such an utterance, both to explain it and to lead it back to its
place—if it has a place, and one that is absolutely its own. (DE,
136/86)
It is clearly vital for Derrida to be able to make some assignment of re-
sponsibility to Heidegger—if not in a naive way by assuming that Hei-
degger is in any straightforward or traditional sense the author of what he
says, but in the sense (which is the only deconstructive sense that can be
made of authorship) that he subscribes, underwrites, appends a signa-
ture to, certain propositions. The story De l’esprit tells is one where an
initial gesture of avoidance of spirit slowly becomes an appropriation,
the initial ghosting of the word through the use of scare quotes return-
ing to haunt Heidegger at or until the end.
I said at the beginning that this looks more like the sort of struc-
ture that informs Derrida’s more “‘critical’’ readings of such authors as
Foucault and Lacan. There, elements are identified which, in spite of
more or less aggressive claims to the contrary by these authors, are taken
by Derrida to show a residual unthought attachment to metaphysics
which in fact contaminates the whole of their thought. Is this where we
are left with “‘Gezst”’ in Heidegger? This is perhaps the most obvious way
to read the book—Heidegger failed in his own “deconstruction” of
metaphysics at least to the extent that he failed to deconstruct this value


90
GEOFFREY
BENNINGTON
of Geist. This is, at any rate, how De Uesprit is read by Lyotard in his little
book on Heidegger et ‘les juifs.’ He summarizes the book quite convinc-
ingly thus:
Jacques Derrida has devoted the resources of the most scrupu-
lous deconstruction to picking out the fate of Geist, geistig, and
geistlich in Heidegger’s text, both philosophical and political.
The demonstration here is blinding, that the action of the rec-
tor is nothing but the knowing in act of the thinker, but that it
has however to give itself the supplement of spirit to find its ef-
fective place and address.
.
.
. Spirit, a region withdrawn from
deconstructive anamnesia, a blind white zone which authorises
a politics that the existential-ontological thinking only
permitted.”
This allows Lyotard to suggest that whatever the excellence of Derrida’s
work in this respect, it amounts simply to a dutiful and filial continuation
where Heidegger left off, seeing that Heidegger himself failed to decon-
struct far enough (leaving this value of spirit unquestioned), and kindly
doing the job for him. Lyotard can then say that that’s all very well, but
that the real ‘“‘fault’’ of Heidegger’s (i.e., his silence after the war on the
death camps) can in no way be explained along these deconstructive
lines. This question, which Lyotard links to an account of “the jews” as a
sort of primarily repressed event in Western culture, cannot according
to him be addressed by deconstruction at all, which is thus more gener-
ally condemned (as it was in Economie libidinale in 1974, and, implicitly at
least, at Cerisy in 1980)° for being pious and nihilistic.
I can’t here reconstruct Lyotard’s arguments with due care. Es-
sentially, he wants to make a radical distinction between the still too
“Greek” Heidegger (still thinking Being) and a “jewish” thinking (think-
ing the Law) which Heidegger, and by extension Derrida, are unable to
think, despite all appearance of an apparently ‘‘jewish” attentiveness to
forgetting, avoiding, complications of origin, and so on.
But after the passages from De l’esprit I’ve just quoted, Derrida
moves on (and Lyotard does not mention this at all) to the idea that
something for Heidegger precedes the question that elsewhere in his work
appeared to be the highest dignity of thought. One of the greatest oddi-
ties of De l’esprit is that this apparently vital point is made largely in a
huge, excessive, footnote, and the oddity is increased by the fact that the
book explicitly states that the footnotes were all added after the delivery
of the text at a conference in 1987, even though it was apparently at an


91
SPILRET*S
SPIRIT
SPIRITS
SPIRIT
earlier conference at Essex that the material for that crucial footnote was
brought to Derrida’s attention, and the note is remarked as being of
unusual importance for a note by being the object of a dedication. One
of the things that this footnote imagines is a “perverse” reading of Hei-
degger that acted literally on all of Heidegger’s recommendations about
crossings-through, avoidance of terms, and so on; one might wonder
how perverse it is to imagine De l’esprit without this footnote, as heard by
its first addressees. That something in Heidegger which precedes the
question (of Being) is not quite the Law in Lyotard’s sense, but some-
thing like a promise, which Derrida would certainly want to say precedes
Law as much as it precedes Being. Further still, and again Lyotard is
silent on this, Derrida locates a “‘foreclosure’”’ (a word Lyotard also uses
in his description of his hypothesized primary repression of ‘“‘the jews” in
Western thought) in Heidegger’s brutal triangulation of the question of
the language of Spirit around the Greek, German, and Latin languages,
and asks: “Does [this triangle] not remain open from its origin and by its
very structure onto what the Greek and Latin had to translate by pneuma
and spiritus, that is, the Hebrew ruah?” (DE, 165/100).
The point here is that if Heidegger is pushing the geistliche in
Trakl toward what ought to be a point of contact with the very Judaic tradi-
tion that Lyotard is claiming Heidegger (and by extension Derrida) just
can’t think, then on the one hand Heidegger’s text would be opening to
deconstruction all his own earlier thinking (or unthinking) about Geist,
but on the other, simultaneously, foreclosing any access to that ‘“‘other”’
tradition by the violent historical delimitation of Greek, Latin, and Ger-
man as the arena in which the matter is to be decided. But allowing that
opening (which is of course quite different from the sort of radical dif-
ference Lyotard has always been pursuing), the textual resources for
which are thus to be found in Heidegger, through the curious imagined
dialogue that ends the book, in which Derrida ghosts, quotes “‘men-
tions,’’ uses and abuses the discourse of all the supposed interlocutors,
at the crossroads at which the location of the archi-originary is no longer
an ontological or transcendental search—allowing that opening shifts
and troubles anything like the opposition I sketched between two sorts
of texts by Derrida, who is here affirming a reinscribed Gevst as Geist’s
ghost, as return of the same as other, repetition as alterity doubling
again, here as everywhere else, as both the “‘theme”’ of deconstructive
writing and its “‘style,” its uses already used as mentions, writing always
also its own ghost. And this is, then, what deconstruction will always have
been saying on its own account, on its own behalf, in its own name, this is
deconstruction’s very signature: Spirit’s Spirit Spirits Spirit—spirit’s


92
GEOFFREY
BENNINGTON
spirit (or ghost ghosts or) spirits (the “‘official’”” metaphysical value of)
spirit (and therefore also the metaphysical value of ghost) which was thus
always already also its own altering repetition, its own ghost, another
name, therefore, for the necessarily odd, duplicitous, haunting and, of
necessity, endlessly resourceful resource of deconstruction itself, its
other.


The Question of the Question:
An Ethico-Political
Response to a Note
In Derrida’s De ‘esprit
Simon Critchley
n order to raise the question of ethical and political responsibility in
Derrida’s work with any immanent necessity, it is at first necessary to
approach that work in its own terms, that is to say, through the textual
practice of deconstruction as it engages in the reading of a specific
text. Such an attention to Derrida’s work, what he described in another
context as “‘an ‘ethico-political duty’ [wn “devoir ethico-politique’],”’ must
be carried out before any compelling assertion might be made about the
ethical or political status of that work. In short, it is necessary to proceed
obliquely. I shall therefore approach the question of deconstruction,
ethics, and politics through a brief reading of Derrida’s Of Spirit; a pro-
foundly self-referential text, I would claim, whose rigor is only out-
matched by its obliqueness. I shall offer a reading of Of Spirit based upon
a long footnote (DE, 147—54/129-36), which has already been the focus
of some discussion,? and which was written as a response to an interven-
tion by Francoise Dastur at the Essex colloquium on “Reading Heideg-
ger” in 1986.° I will try to show how this footnote sets the agenda for a
re-reading of Heidegger and—more obliquely and perhaps more impor-
93


94
SIMON
CRTC Y
tantly—of Derrida, a reading specifically in terms of the question of ethi-
cal and political responsibility.
Derrida’s ‘‘hypothesis” in Of Spirit is the following:
such at least is my hypothesis—to recognize in it [Heidegger’s
interpretation of the word Spirit, Geist], in its very equivoca-
tion or indecision [indécision—my emphasis], the edging or divid-
ing path [le chemin de bordure ou de partage| which ought,
according to Heidegger pass between a Greek or Christian—
even onto-theological—determination of pneuma or spiritus,
and a thinking of Geist which would be other and more origi-
nary. (DE, 128/81-82)
According to Derrida, Heidegger’s thinking moves between two deter-
minations of Spirit, one belonging to ontotheology or metaphysics, that
of the 1933 Rectoral Address or the 1935 An Introduction to Metaphysics,
which Heidegger had said ought to be avoided early in Sein und Zeit, and
the other pointing toward a more originary and nonmetaphysical think-
ing that appears most forcibly in the 1953 essay on Trakl, ““Language in
the Poem.’’* The movement of Heidegger’s thinking oscillates indeci-
sively between these two possibilities and it is this very indecision that
fascinates Derrida: ““That’s what I like about Heidegger. When I think
about him, when I read him, I’m aware of both these vibrations at
the same time” (DE, 109/68). Heidegger’s thinking moves relentlessly
between two borders, the metaphysical and the nonmetaphysical, alter-
nately striking both edges and producing a dissonant resonance. How-
ever, the undecidability of such an experience of reading and Derrida’s
consequent refusal to choose one determination of Spirit rather than
the other, does not agnostically sidestep a confrontation with ethics and
politics; rather it provides a space where the latter can be addressed. My
claim is that it is precisely in the suspension of choice or decision be-
tween two alternatives, a suspension provoked in, as, and through a
practice of double reading, that the ethical dimension of deconstruction
is opened and maintained. I argue in The Ethics of Deconstruction that an
unconditional duty or affirmation is the source of the injunction that
produces deconstruction, and that the textual practice of double read-
ing keeps open a dimension of alterity or transcendence that has ethical
significance.°
In his reading of “Language in the Poem,’ Derrida comes across
the word “‘versprechender,” “more promising” (US, 77/OL, 194). This
leads him to focus on the notion of the promise (la promesse, das Ver-


95
THE
QUESTION
OF
THE
QUESTION
sprechen), and occasions a short digression into Paul de Man’s reading of
Rousseau’s Social Contract in Allegories of Reading, where, at the end of a
discussion of the nature of the promise, de Man rewrites Heidegger’s
formula die Sprache spricht (language speaks) as die Sprache verspricht (sich)
(language promises [itself]) (DE, 146 /93).° With this minimal and seem-
ingly contingent digression, Derrida notes that he has perhaps, ‘“‘left the
order of commentary, if something like that exists” (DE, 146-47 /94).
However, as he leaves the order of commentary, the indispensable mo-
ment of repetition where the dominant interpretation of the text is re-
constructed (L/, 265/Litr, 143), Derrida moves on to the question of the
question which forms the subtitle of Of Spirit. Heidegger and the Question.
What is the question of the question? Derrida writes, “It remains to be
known if this Versprechen is not the promise which, opening every speak-
ing, makes possible the very question and therefore precedes it without
belonging to it: the dissymmetry of an affirmation, of a yes before all
opposition of yes and no”’ (DE, 147/94). The thought here concerns the
possibility of a promise that would render questioning possible without,
however, belonging to the order of interrogation. It is the question of a
promise, a moment of affirmation that Derrida has elsewhere described
in the language of Kant’s ethics as an unconditional categorical impera-
tive (LI, 281—82/LItr, 152-53). As if to clearly signal this departure from
the repetitive order of commentary, Derrida opens an eight-page foot-
note, which deals at length with the question of the question and which, I
would claim, opens both the ultimately ethical orientation of Of Spirit and
provides the key to Derrida’s reading of Heidegger.
Derrida’s most general concern in this footnote, then, is to eluci-
date a form of language that would precede and render possible all ques-
tioning of the form: What is x? That is to say, all ontological questioning,
insofar as every question asks after the essence or Being of an entity. The
centrality of the question (die Frage) to Heidegger’s thinking is apparent
from as early as the Introduction to Sein und Zeit, where it is the question
of the meaning of Being (die Frage nach dem Sinn von Sein), that has today
been forgotten, which must be formulated and submitted to repetition
or recapitulation (Wiederholung). And where the project of fundamental
ontology is organized according to the tripartite schema of that which is
asked about in any investigation (das Gefragte, namely, Being), that which
is to be interrogated (das Befragte, namely, Dasein), and that which is to
found out by the asking (das Erfragte, namely, the meaning of Being). The
very possibility of fundamental ontology is conditional upon Dasein’s
distinctive relationship with the question of Being. If Dasein can raise
the question of Being or if Being is an issue for Dasein, albeit initially


96
SYMON
CRUTCH
DE ¥
only in a vague and average manner, then this shows that Dasein has an
understanding of Being (Seinsverstdndnis) and that Sein und Zeit can, lit-
erally and logically, begin. Sein und Zeit begins by showing the necessity
(paragraph 1), the structure (paragraph 2), and the ontic and ontological
priority (paragraphs 3 and 4) of the Seinsfrage, and the way or method
(paragraphs 5—7) that will be followed in the elaboration or working out
(Ausarbeitung) of this question. Heidegger’s thought begins from the ne-
cessity of making the question of Being a question once again for us, as it
was for the Stranger in Plato’s Sophist. It is questioning that will grasp the
oblivion of Being as oblivion.
Now, some twenty-five years after the publication of Sein und Zeit,
in the 1953 lecture ““The Question Concerning Technology,” Heidegger
would appear to maintain this priority of questioning when the latter is
characterized as the piety of thinking (Denn das Fragen ist die Frommigkeit
des Denkens).’ However, despite this undoubted priority, Derrida (at the
prompting of Frangoise Dastur) locates
a moment in Heidegger where
the question itself undergoes an inversion or reversal (Umkehrung: US,
176/OL, 72; Bouleversement: DE, 150/131). In the course of the three
lectures that comprise “The Nature of Language’’ (Das Wesen der
Sprache: US, 158-238/OL, 57-108), and which originally date from
1957-1958, Heidegger rereads his statement that questioning is the
piety of thought and adds,
The lecture ending with that sentence was already in the ambi-
ance of the realization that the true stance of thinking cannot
be to put questions, but must be listening to the grant or
pledge. [daf die eigentliche Gebdrde des Denkens nicht das Fragen
sein kann, sondern das Horen der Zusage dessen sein muf.| (US,
176/OL, 72)
It is with this notion of the Zusage (in French gage: pledge or grant, as in
mortgage, literally a death pledge) that Derrida locates the Umkehrung in
Heidegger’s thinking. Prior to the putting of questions to language (i.e.,
what is the essence of language?), it is clear that language has already
been granted (schon zugesprochen sein: US, 175/OL, 71). All questioning
requires the prior pledge or Zusage of that which is put in question.
Thus, for the later Heidegger, the primary datum of language is das
Horen der Zusage, listening to the grant or pledge. As is clear from Der-
rida’s 1987 text on Michel de Certeau, the Zusage is a moment of affir-
mation in Heidegger’s text, “‘in short, a yes.’’8
Derrida then proceeds to draw out the implications of this inver-


97
THE
QUESTION
OF
THE
QUESTION
sion, tracing it back across some dominant moments in Heidegger’s
work. What is significant about the Zusage is that all forms of questioning
are always already pledged (gagé) to respond to a prior grant of lan-
guage. The question and the questioning stance of philosophy is always a
response to and a responsibility for that which is prior and over which
the question has no priority: “It [the question] responds in advance,
whatever it does, to this pledge and of this pledge [4 ce gage et de ce gage].
It is engaged by it in a responsibility it has not chosen [qu’elle n’a pas
choiste—my emphasis] and which assigns it even its liberty’ (DE,
148—49/130). What is primary in language is that to which one is respon-
sible and which has not been chosen. The liberty and choice of the ques-
tioning attitude are subordinated to a prior responsibility. The origin of
language is responsibility (DE, 151/132). My language begins as a re-
sponse to the Other. In short, in Levinasian terms, it is ethical.
In this footnote, then, Derrida is proposing nothing less than an
agenda for the re-reading of Heidegger, which the latter’s readers are
now better prepared to recognize and which arises as a response to the
questions of politics and ethics, a response made all the more urgent by
lVaffaire Heidegger. Derrida continues,
This is useful not only for reading Heidegger [emphasized in
the French—lire Heidegger—and an allusion to the Essex Col-
loquium of the same name] and serving some hermeneutical or
philological piety. Beyond an always indispensable exegesis, this
re-reading sketches out another topology for new tasks, for
what remains to be situated of the relationships between Hei-
degger’s thought and other places of thought—or of the en-
gage [de l’engage|—places which one pictures as regions but which
are not (ethics or politics .
. .). (DE, 151/132-33—-my emphasis)
After the inversion of Heidegger’s thinking that the thought of the
Zusage offers, the task of re-reading would not simply content itself with
the hermeneutic piety of reading Heidegger, but would re-read Heideg-
ger’s corpus both in terms of excavating the thought of responsibility
within it (Derrida himself lays down several markers for the itinerary of
such a rereading: DE, 151—52/133), and in rethinking the relation be-
tween “regional ontologies,” like ethics and politics, and the truth of
Being.
Of Spirit is an almost “‘classical’’ example of a deconstructive read-
ing—the strictest and most rigorous determination of figures of oscilla-
tion or undecidability in a text—which shows how the thought of


98
SIMON
CRITCHLEY
responsibility emerges in such a reading. Derrida’s text is a response,
both to the highly critical readings of Heidegger’s political involvement,’
and, more importantly and self-referentially, to the accusation that his
own work (so often caricatured as ‘‘l’Heideggerianisme francais’’)!° sim-
ply avoids discussions of ethical and political responsibility and that
deconstruction leads to either an amoral anarchism or a depoliticized
quietism. On a more sinister level, another version of the same polemic
might argue that Derrida’s work is indeed political, but because of its
‘“‘Heideggerianism’’—and Heidegger’s thinking was, as everyone (das
Man) now knows with complete assurance, “fascist right down to its most
intimate components’’!!—deconstruction necessarily entails a fascist
politics. This sort of argument, or rather assertion, is surprisingly pro-
posed by Manfred Frank and quoted approvingly by Habermas in his
preface to the German edition of Farias’s book. After having lauded
Habermas’s The Philosophical Discourse of Modernity, Frank writes,
The new-French theories are taken up by many of our students
like a gospel.
.
.
. I think the phenomenon is frightening, be-
cause it seems to me that young Germans are sucking back in,
under the pretence of an opening to what is French and inter-
national, their own irrationalist tradition, which had been bro-
ken off after the Third Reich.!”
One might infer from this kind of statement that Derrida’s avoidance of
politics is merely a dissimulation of the textual fascism implicit in decon-
structive reading. I noted above that Of Spirit begins with the question of
avoidance in Heidegger. In the light of the above political accusations
which crudely infer ‘‘the politics of deconstruction” from what is curso-
rily assumed to be the truth of Heidegger’s political engagement (‘‘Hei-
degger is a Nazi, Derrida is Heideggerian, therefore Derrida is a Nazi’’),
it should now be asked: Who or what is being charged with avoidance in
Of Spirit? Who or what is being defended? Is Of Spirit a defense of Hei-
degger? Or is it Derrida’s self-defense before a political tribunal?
I would claim that Of Spirit is an elaborate act of ventriloquism,
whose subject is necessarily double, where Derrida addresses the ques-
tion of ethical and political responsibility in his own work through a
reading of Heidegger. It is, after all, claimed that the semantic ambiva-
lence of the word Geist and its cognates in Heidegger’s work ‘“‘perhaps
decides the very meaning of the political as such” (DE, 19/6). However,
the obliqueness of the latter remark—and indeed of Of Spirit as a
whole—is necessary to Derrida’s response. I would claim for Derrida


99
THE
QUESTION
oF
THE
QUESTION
that which he claims about Heidegger in the short 1987 text ‘Comment
donner raison,” namely that the immediate presentation of a question or a
problem is a disaster for thinking and all that Derrida is seeking to do in
Of Spirit is to keep open the possibility of thinking in spite of the disas-
ter—the disaster that would forbid the reading of Heidegger or Derrida
in the name of some political purification process. For Derrida, ‘‘the
vigilant but open reading of Heidegger remains in my eyes one of the
indispensable conditions, one of them but not the least, for trying to
comprehend better and to tell better why, with so many others, I have
always condemned Nazism.”’!’ It is not by prohibiting the reading of Hei-
degger and Derrida that the threat of fascism is avoided. Indeed, quite
the opposite might be true.
Of Spirit, then, deconstructively responds to both the reductive
critique of Heidegger’s politics and to the supposed avoidance of politics
or quasi fascism of Derrida’s own work. It is a response which has as its
horizon the thought of responsibility, of language as a response to the
Other. By posing the question of the question, Derrida asks if question-
ing is indeed at the origin of thinking and, consequently, if all thinking is,
in a Levinasian sense, ontological. The question of the question chal-
lenges the priority of ontology and finds in language an irreducible di-
mension of responsibility. To quote Levinas,
If the question what? in its adherence to Being, is at the origin
of all thinking . .
. all research and all philosophy go back to
ontology.
.
.
. But the question of the Question is more radical.
Why does research take the form of a question? How is it that
the what? already plunged in Being so as to open it up the
more, becomes a demand [demande] and prayer [priére], a par-
ticular language inserting into the communication of the given
[donné], a call for help, for aid, addressed to the Other
[autruz]?!*
Prior to all questioning, deconstruction opens a dimension of responsi-
bility—grant, pledge, prayer, demand, call, Saying—that precedes on-
tology and which puts me into relation with the Other.
On my brief reading of Of Spirit, such would indeed appear to be
Derrida’s response to the question of ethical and political responsibility
in Heidegger’s and his own work. Furthermore, this would indeed seem
to be the only deconstructive response that Derrida could make to this
question. He responds to insinuation and polemic by producing a quite
exemplary deconstructive reading of Heidegger. What more could one


100
SIMON
CRITCHLEY
expect from Derrida? After all, could one reasonably expect Derrida’s
work not to be Derridian?
I would claim, further, that Derrida’s response to Heidegger’s
politics typifies the deconstructive gesture with regard to the question of
ethical and political responsibility; namely, “‘there can be no moral or politi-
cal responsibility without this trial and without this passage by way of the
undecidable”’ (LI, 210/LItr, 116—my emphasis). One finds this gesture
repeated throughout Derrida’s writings on political topics, and to
choose two further examples, almost at random, I will quickly look at
Derrida’s essay on Paul de Man’s politics, ““Like the Sound of the Sea
Deep within a Shell,’’'® and a recent text on the problem of European
identity, ““L’autre cap.’’!® The former text turns entirely on the question
of responsibility and of how one is to respond to the revelations of Paul
de Man’s wartime journalism. Derrida’s reading of de Man is dominated
by the double rhythm of ‘‘on the one hand” and ‘‘on the other hand”
(MPM, 169-70/LSS, 607). On the one hand, de Man’s wartime journal-
ism seems to correspond to the official rhetoric of the occupying Ger-
man forces in Belgium. However, on the other hand, Derrida claims that
a closer reading shows how that rhetoric is brought into question and
contradicted. De Man’s texts are thus enclosed within a double bind that
does not allow a simple “‘décision de justice’ (MPM, 215/LSS, 643) to be
taken with regard to any alleged complicity with National Socialism. This
logic of the double bind is extended (and perhaps even stretched to
breaking point) to a reading of de Man’s most damning article, “Les Juifs
dans la littérature actuelle.’”’!7 On the one hand, de Man seems to give his
consent to the plan to rid Europe of “the Jewish Problem” by “‘the cre-
ation of a Jewish colony isolated from Europe,” which “‘would not entail,
for the literary life of the West, deplorable consequences” (MPM, 190/
LSS, 623). However, on the other hand, Derrida claims, audaciously,
that this text itself proceeds toward a demystification or critique of “‘vul-
gar anti-semitism” (MPM, 191-—92/LSS, 624).
Similarly, in “L’autre cap,” Derrida claims that any responsible
notion of European identity must obey a double and contradictory im-
perative (p. 13). To be European means obeying a duty to both recall
what Europe is or was, whilst at the same time opening Europe to the
non-European, welcoming the foreigner in his or her alterity. It is a dou-
ble duty to criticize both totalitarianism and the new religion of Capital
that threatens to take its place. It is a duty to respect both difference and
singularity, whilst at the same time maintaining the universality of law,
for example the necessity for international law.'8 Therefore, to be re-
sponsibly European is to experience the undecidability of a double bind.


THE
QUESTION
OF
THE
QUESTION
Thus, deconstructive reading responds to political topics by giv-
ing a rigorous double reading of a text and by showing how the un-
decidability of reading has its horizon in the thought of irreducible
responsibility, an affirmation of alterity; in The Ethics of Deconstruction, I
demonstrate the Levinasian ethical significance of the latter. The ques-
tion that remains—and it is precisely the way in which the question re-
mains that I want to pursue in a series of concluding questions—is
whether Derrida’s approach is an adequate response to politics. If, as has
been shown in Of Spirit, the ethical moment in deconstruction asks the
question of the question, the primacy of responsibility to the Other, then
is there not also a necessary moment of questioning that would be
directly related to politics? Is not politics precisely the domain of ques-
tioning, that is, of contestation, antagonism, struggle, conflict, and dis-
sension? What is the relation, in Derrida’s work, between the rigorous
and responsible undecidability of deconstructive reading and the neces-
sity for political decisions and political critique? To adapt a remark of
Blanchot’s—and I am thinking in particular of the essay on de Man—is
there not a point where the practice of an art becomes an insult to the
victims?!
Deconstruction can certainly be employed as a powerful means of
political analysis. For example, to show how a certain dominant political
régime—Apartheid, say—is based upon a set of undecidable presup-
positions is an important step in the subversion of that régime’s claim to
legitimacy.*° Showing, as Ernesto Laclau has done, how the terrain of
the social does not attain closure, but is an ever incomplete, undecidable
structure, is a crucial step in the subversion of totalizing conceptions of
society and the development of new political strategies.?! But how is one
to account for the move from undecidability to the political decision to
combat that domination? If deconstruction is the strictest possible deter-
mination of undecidability in the limitless context of, for want of a better
word, “experience,” then this entails a suspension of the moment of de-
cision. Yet, decisions have to be taken. But how? And in virtue of what?
How does one make a decision in an undecidable terrain? I would claim,
with Laclau, that an adequate account of the decision is essential to the
possibility of politics, and that it is precisely this that deconstruction does
not provide.”? As an epigraph to his celebrated essay on Foucault, Der-
rida cites Kierkegaard, “The instant of the decision is madness.”’** This is
a good statement of the deconstructive dilemma: one has to make deci-
sions, yet the moment of the decision is madness. I take a risk one way or
another, I am for x or against x, but ultimately I do not know why I made
this decision. I can no longer ground my political decisions upon some


102
SIMON
CRT CHL EY
ontological basis or eidos, or upon a set of a priori principles or proce-
dures. Now, this may well be true, perhaps the ground for one’s political
decisions is ultimately contingent, but is the madness of the decision it-
self an adequate account of political life? Is it even a valid description of
how one forms one’s political preferences and engages in political ac-
tion? If this were so, then might not Richard Rorty finally have a point in
finding in Derrida’s work a quest for ironical, private perfection which is
politically useless and perhaps even pernicious?**
To summarize, is the account of politics given by Derrida either
too formalistic and abstract at the level of undecidability, or too contin-
gent and empty at the level of the decision? More gravely, in the rigorous
and quasi-transcendental delineation of undecidability as the dimension
of political responsibility, is there not an implicit refusal of the ontic, the
factical and the empirical, that is to say, the space of doxa, where politics
takes place in a field of antagonism, decision, dissension, and struggle?
In this sense, might one not ultimately speak of a refusal of politics in
Derrida’s work?


Spirit’s
Living Hand
Will McNeill
n his introductory remarks to De l’esprit, Derrida points to what he
sees as “‘the problem” with Heidegger’s appeals to Geist. The problem
is that of an authority which would be associated with Heidegger’s
employment of the word Geist—of a certain unquestioned authority:
An extra-ordinary authority returns [revient] to this motif of
spirit or of the spiritual in its German language. Precisely to the
extent that it does not appear at the forefront of the scene, it
seems to withdraw [se soustraire] from any destruction or
deconstruction, as though it did not belong [appartenir] to a
history of ontology—and this will be precisely the problem.
(DE, 18/5)
Where Heidegger might have succumbed to a temptation to protect,
however surreptitiously, the rhetoric of Geist from the project of a de-
struction (Destruktion) of the history of ontology, Derrida would there-
fore submit this rhetoric to the relentless questioning of his own
deconstruction. And if Derrida’s suspicion were shown to be justified,
then this undertaking would undoubtedly be urgently necessary—not
least on account of the political implications surrounding Heidegger’s
appeals to Geist. Nothing must be allowed to escape the unswaying vigi-
lance of the deconstructive eye. Yet what is the price of such vigilance of
103


104
WILL
McNEILL
questioning? Does not this vigilance have its own ghosts, and perhaps
necessarily so? Does there not come a moment when deconstructive
questioning must itself withdraw from the scene? And how are we to
interpret this withdrawal? In particular, does not such questioning inevi-
tably risk appearing as the master discourse, the authoritative discourse,
the authority on all possible authority—and thereby risk the return of
precisely that which it would deconstruct?
Such aporia are familiar enough by now, and I do not mean to
suggest that Derrida is unaware of these problems. On the contrary, this
is precisely what interests him in De l’esprit. The above quotation indi-
cates clearly enough that what will be at stake in any reflection on these
issues is the interwovenness of appearing and not appearing (Gevst in
Heidegger’s text fails to appear “‘at the forefront of the scene’’; yet pre-
cisely in not appearing at the forefront it nonetheless appears, an-
nounces itself, ‘“‘seems to withdraw’’), of withdrawal (se sowstraire) and
belonging (appartenir). Derrida, in analyzing this play of appearing yet
not appearing, belonging yet withdrawing, focuses much of his interest
on such play as written, as inscribed in “‘the law of quotation marks”’ (DE,
53/31).' Such textual marks, of course, do not appear in speaking. Or if
they do, it is inevitably via an indirect reference to textuality. In any Case,
it is difficult to imagine Heidegger raising his hands to gesture quotation
marks around ‘‘Geist’’ when delivering his Rectoral Address or the lecture
course An Introduction to Metaphysics. Such a gesture would have ap-
peared rather comical, to say the least, especially given the frequency
with which Gest manifested itself in those oral deliveries. On the other
hand, in a certain sense everything spoken is surrounded by quotation
marks precisely in its being spoken. And what appears or is pointed or
gestured toward in and through speaking always withdraws or threatens
to withdraw much more directly and immediately in being spoken.? The
quotation marks as textualized are therefore perhaps not merely in-
tended to warn. They are not merely ‘‘scare quotes,”’ as we call them, but
through the text gesture toward what the text itself, once inscribed, can-
not say: toward, that is, a fragility of the word, toward the moment of the
writing, reading, or speaking of whatever is written, read, or spoken.
In the following remarks I will pursue a suspicion concerning De
lesprit: that in focusing on the play of appearance and withdrawal as
written, as textualized, Derrida is perhaps insufficiently attentive to the
writing of what is written, to the way in which the living hand that writes
is drawn across the page, and to possible resources in Heidegger’s texts
for thinking such fragility. This is not intended simply as a criticism of
Derrida, nor indeed of the necessity of the deconstructive moves made


105
SVR eT
ss
LIVING
HAND
by De lVesprit. On the contrary. I am interested, rather, in the way in
which such a deconstructive reading sets out its own theater, the struc-
tural framework it erects for its own arguments, the premises and
presuppositions it depends upon—presuppositions which may be both
necessary to and necessitate a deconstructive reading. For example, why
is Derrida so sure that there is a Jaw of quotation marks in Heidegger’s
texts? Is he sure of this? What if the very assumption of such a law—and
perhaps of law itself in its “‘extra-ordinary” manifestation and author-
ity—were merely an effect of textuality, a particular kind of semblance
to which textuality might give rise? My suspicion, in short, concerns a
peculiar certainty which, as Derrida himself concedes, governs his read-
ing of Heidegger in De l’esprit. This certainty is a ‘‘negative”’ certainty. It
is a certainty nonetheless: “the certainty of not fully understanding what
ultimately regulates Heidegger’s spiritual idiom” (DE, 30/12). Why, and
with what right, does Derrida presuppose that there is such an ultimate
regulation? At the very least, his negative certainty is premised on the
possibility of such a regulation, a possibility which must guide his entire
reading, even if that reading itself shows any such regulation to be elu-
sive or indeed lacking. Yet who other than Heidegger has taught us to be
suspicious of presupposing such an arche?
I shall return to the question of this regulative presupposition of
Derrida’s own enquiry in a moment. To begin with, however, let us note
that the search for whatever governs Heidegger’s thinking of Gezst is ar-
ticulated around four “‘guiding threads”’ identified by Derrida: the
themes of questioning, technology, animality, and epochality. Yet within
each of these guiding threads Derrida proceeds to institute—not bru-
tally, but insistently nevertheless—division and difference. There is,
firstly, the difference between the question and “something other”’ (au-
tre chose) than the question; secondly, the difference between technology
and the essence of technology; thirdly, the difference or “‘opposition”’
between the animal and human Dasein; finally, “‘the difference
.
.
. be-
tween the Platonic-Christian, metaphysical or onto-theological determi-
nation of the spiritual (geistig) and another thinking of the spiritual”
(DE, 29/12).
Let us note, furthermore, the language used by Derrida to de-
scribe such oppositions and differences: in three out of the four cases it is
a language of the shelter (abri) or sheltering (abriter). For example in the
Derridian suspicion that the privilege of questioning in Heidegger re-
mains, almost always, sheltered (4 l’abri). Sheltered, indeed, neither by nor
from a question, but from something else (DE, 25/9). Or in the accusation
that, at least in one respect, the statement “the essence of technology is


106
WILL
McNEILL
nothing technological’”’ maintains or holds on to (maintient) a thought of
essence and thus of questioning ‘“‘sheltered or protected from [4 l’abri de]
any originary and essential contamination by technology” (DE, 26/10).
What is this sheltering? It is above all, Derrida suggests, a sheltering of
Geist, for which Heidegger wants to save an “interior purity,’ a purity
sheltered from all destitution or Entmachtung. Or again, the claim that
Heidegger’s discourse on animality leaves “sheltered [abrités] in obscu-
rity” axioms of the most profound metaphysical humanism (DE, 28/12).
In the fourth case, as we have just seen, Derrida simply speaks of a “‘differ-
ence’’ between one “determination”’ of Geist and another. It amounts to
the same thing; ‘‘to shelter” here invariably has the sense of ‘‘to delimit,”
“to confine,” “to close off,”’ in any case a form of closure.
This instituting appeal to difference appears to me to be some-
what precipitous. It is also an eminently metaphysical gesture. (Has
metaphysics ever sought anything other than to delimit?) And once dif-
ference and division has been installed within each of these four threads,
the delimitations presupposed will organize Derrida’s own analyses in De
l’esprit. The analyses can then go on to show—rejoining Heidegger in
their own way—how spirit returns as something “quite other” to disrupt
such oppositional delimitations. Yet does not Derrida, at the very mo-
ment he begins to write, then fall prey to the same move which he will
proceed to impute to Heidegger, a move belonging to the ‘‘rhetorical
strategy’ of ‘‘avoiding while not avoiding’’? (‘‘This un-avoidance
[znévitement], to be sure, now supposes and will henceforth maintain a
prior delimitation [a délimitation antérieure]”” [DE, 44/23].) Is De esprit
itself an exercise in how to avoid?
What, then, if certain resources in Heidegger’s texts already dis-
rupted such oppositional delimitations which constitute—however provi-
sionally (for provisionality is always a looking ahead which governs what is
to come, avenir)*>—Derrida’s point of departure? I suspect that this thesis
could be substantiated for each of the four threads Derrida identifies
(though with greatest difficulty, no doubt, in the case of animality). Here,
however, I shall merely attempt to follow up this suspicion of a possible
disruptive moment with respect to one of the four threads: that of the
question and its privilege. I shall then examine how one might—again
with the aid of resources from Heidegger’s texts—begin to problematize
Derrida’s writing of spirit, his writing De l’esprit, in terms of a disruption
which perhaps could not simply be read off its textual inscription (whether
that inscription has recourse to quotation marks or not).
Let me begin with the question of the question and its sheltering.
Let me begin at the place where the “question of all questions,” the


SBERRT’ Ss
LIVING
HAND
question of Being itself begins: not in Sein wnd Zeit, but in an earlier
presentation of the beginning of that treatise, namely the 1925 course
Prolegomena to the History of the Concept of Time. Now Derrida claims that
Dasein, the “‘we’’ that one must avoid calling “spirit,” is ‘‘at first and
solely [d’abord et seulement] determined starting from our opening onto
the question of Being’’; as thus inscribed (telle quelle s’inscrit) (namely both
as questioner and object of interrogation: as der Frager and das Befragte)
in the network of questioning (Fragen), Dasein will have received both its
privilege and its ‘‘primary, minimal and most certain determination.” A
reflexivity of questioning would thus determine in advance the point of
departure of the existential analytic of Dasein. Questioning itself could
not be questioned, without confirming it ‘‘a priori and in a circular man-
ner.” An “a priori” privilege of questioning would thereby remain shel-
tered from the point of departure (le point de départ) of the analytic of
Dasein (DE, 36-37/17-18).
Is this the vicious circle—strenuously denied by Heidegger in
Sein und Zeit—returning to haunt him? Yet a careful reading of the 1925
course might suggest that matters are not quite so straightforward.
What, Heidegger there asks, are the grounds for posing the question of
Being? Why the question, what are the grounds for this Fragen? Not,
precisely not, the possibility of questioning. To quote Heidegger: ‘“The
sole grounds for the possibility of the question of Being in general is
Dasein itself as possible being [Moglichsein], its discoveredness |Entdecktheit]
in possibilities” (GA 20, 184—85/136). Being possible, possibility pure
and simple, then, and not the determinate or determining possibility of
questioning. Far from being sheltered in the circularity of its unques-
tioned privilege, then, questioning itself might already in its beginning be
unsettled, unsettled by something irreducible to the possibility of ques-
tioning. What is this unsettling to which questioning might here be ex-
posed? Questioning (Fragen) itself is a being (Seiendes), says Heidegger,
that being which is explicitly given with [mit] the question, yet
at the same time also given in such a way that it in the first
place and above all is overlooked precisely in the course of ques-
tioning [im Zuge des Fragens]. Here the attempt is to be made
not to overlook precisely this being from the commencement,
not to overlook it precisely with respect to questioning con-
cerning Being itself. (GA 20, 200/148—emphasis added)
This being, questioning, is not to be overlooked. And this caution
is to be exercised not in respect of its Being—the determinate Being of


108
WILL
McNEILL
the question—but in respect of questioning concerning Being itself. This
means, precisely, not overlooking “‘the course of questioning,”’ den Zug
des Fragens: that is, the draw or trait of questioning (or perhaps the breath
of questioning: the German can mean all of these). The suspicion, in
effect, is this: What if questioning, as a possibility of Dasein—question-
ing thus inscribed as what is interrogated (das Befragte) in the very point
of departure of the existential analytic—what if this questioning and this
inscription for a possible reading were itself already in dispersion?* What,
in sum, if “‘we,’’ Dasein, were not “‘determined at first and solely [d’abord
et seulement determinés| from our opening to the question of Being,”’ but
from our opening onto a horizon which would exceed any determinate
question or structure of questioning? When Derrida writes: ““The point
of departure in the existential analytic is legitimated at first and solely
[d’abord et seulement] from the possibility, experience, structure and reg-
ulated modifications of questioning’ (DE, 36/17), he is certainly cor-
rect—if by “point of departure” he is referring to what Heidegger calls
the Ausgang of the analytic. Yet Derrida appears to confuse this point of
departure with, and indeed reduce it to, the beginning or Ansatz of the
analytic, to which he also refers in translating it likewise as point de départ:
point of departure, and in attempting to turn against Heidegger ‘‘what
he himself says” (DE, 37/18). These two moments (as I have tried to
argue in detail elsewhere)? are not the same. Whereas point of departure
or Ausgang refers to the purely formal determination of a regulated
structure of questioning, beginning or Ansaéz entails the moment of fac-
tical involvement on the part of the questioner: what Heidegger terms
the engagement (Einsatz) of Dasein in its concretion. The point of depar-
ture, to be sure, belongs to such beginning, yet is not reducible to it. The
beginning would exceed any determinate or regulated structure of ques-
tioning, in a word: any regulative inscription. In the moment, in the
course or breath of questioning, it would perhaps point toward an open-
ing which would exceed and somehow precede questioning thus in-
scribed—toward what Heidegger (with extraordinary caution, even by
his standards) in the 1925 lecture course identifies as an indeterminate
preunderstanding out of which the question of Being first arises. The in-
determinacy at issue here would, needless to say, not be the symmetrical
counterpart of a determinacy pertaining to the question (for this would
already be to determine it). It would, presumably, point toward the
appeal or address of language itself which Derrida comments on in a
subsequent footnote.®
Far from being sheltered in its privilege from the point of depar-
ture of the analytic of Dasein, questioning, therefore, might already be


109
SPIRITS
LIVING
HAND
disrupted in its very beginning, drawn to acknowledge its own disper-
sion. And if that dispersion were not without a certain Versammlung or
gathering—were not without its Geist7—-such gathering might have to be
thought very differently than in terms of regulation or law. It would be a
gathering “‘quite other.”’
It seems, then, that Derrida’s confusion of point of departure and
beginning leads him to “‘overlook”’ (to use Heidegger’s word) a possible
unsettling of questioning and its alleged privilege announced in Heideg-
ger’s texts as early as, and even earlier than, Sein und Zeit. This enables
him to set up a scenario which installs difference and delimitation within
the moment of questioning—as though the “‘something else”’ sheltering
and bestowing the privilege of the question were simply other than the
question, as though Geist were a determinate other whose regulative
moves could be traced.
If the Zug of questioning—the course of questioning, its direc-
tional movement or draw—is overlooked by Derrida here, or at least
thought reductively, in terms of possible determinancy (and perhaps
necessarily so), how might we begin to think the disruption announced
by such a Zug? What would its implications be for the writing of De
esprit? I shall now attempt to approach this issue along the lines of the
hand, a theme which has occupied Derrida’s reading of Heidegger else-
where, yet which must continue to haunt De l’esprit—even where it does
not appear at the forefront of the scene. This issue—as Derrida himself
notes*—is not only inscribed within the third thread of animality, but
potentially permeates all four threads identified at the beginning of a
treatise. The theme of the hand, as I shall indicate in a moment, thus
bears on the nexus or gathering (Versammlung) of the four threads, in
other words on what Derrida is attempting to trace as Geust.
Can we identify the main trait of De l’esprit? What is De Vesprit
about? What is De l’esprit of? Derrida says:
In this title, De l’esprit, the Franco-Latin de also announces
that, in the classical form of the enquiry, or of the dissertation,
I would like to begin to treat de l’esprit, of spirit, of the concept
and of the word, of the terms Geist, geistig, geistlich in Heideg-
ger. (DE, 17/45)
This treatise, then, in the classical form of an “‘enquiry,”’ that is to say, a
questioning, is to treat (¢razter) of spirit. But what is the trait of this trea-
tise, what is its draw? What is it that draws this treatise into its enquiry? By
what is it drawn? This treatise is not simply ‘‘about”’ spirit. It is of spirit.


110
WILL
McNEILL
Or rather, of esprit. Or rather, of Geist. Et cetera. And all the rest. De
lesprit is not simply about translation: Written as it is in at least six lan-
guages, it is itself translation. Yet are we to say on this account that it is
without 2s trait, its draw, its Versammlung?® In any case, De l’esprit, by its
very title, is inevitably drawn into and held by the very subject-matter of
which it would treat. It cannot, ultimately, avoid the return of that which
might possibly withdraw from it, disrupting it in the very moment of its
questioning. At issue, then, will be a withdrawal accompanying the hand
that writes, the hand that writes of spirit. It will be a question, once
more, of draw and withdrawal; in French, of trait and se soustraire; in
German, of Zug and Entziehen.
As with questioning and its structure, Heidegger’s 1925 course
may suggest other possible readings of Sein und Zeit and of later texts
where the question of the hand is concerned. The theme of the hand
arises in the context of an analysis of world and of Dasein’s ontological
constitution as being-in-the-world. Knowing (Erkennen), like question-
ing, is said to be a founded mode of being-in-the-world. Any epistemol-
ogy which overlooks this ontological configuration in positing Dasein
as a subject which subsequently enters into a relation to an object ‘‘de-
naturalizes”’ (denaturiert) Dasein. Yet how does Heidegger here
renaturalize Dasein, making visible what it means to be in a world? As-
tonishingly, by way of analogy with a certain being to which, ‘‘formally
speaking”’ (he notes parenthetically), ‘“‘we must likewise ascribe the
kind of Being of Dasein—'life’ (GA 20, 223/165).!° Which being, pre-
cisely? A creepy kind of being, a being with but one single foot that
does not leave the earth, with nothing resembling hands or Greiforgane,
and with its eyes, which it has been unable to contain, outgrown on
stalks.
A being which, moreover, carries its own house with it: A snail,
of course.
.
.
. Now this creature, says Heidegger, is not in its shell or
housing as water is ‘‘in’’ a glass; rather, ‘‘it has the inside of its house as
world which it pushes against [sich stoft], in which it warms itself, and
the like.” The snail does not first come in contact with the world by
leaving its shell. “It only creeps out insofar as it is already in a world in
accordance with its Being. It does not first acquire a world by touch-
ing, rather it touches because its Being means nothing other than be-
ing in a world” (GA 20, 224/166).
The analogy is astonishing, not least because Heidegger will sub-
sequently resist any straightforward equation of Dasein with life. But
what is meant here by touching, Tasten? Following the analogy, Dasein’s
primary relation to world is not that of touching. If Dasein is not Geist as
traditionally understood in its distinction from the body, Dasein is no


SPVURUT’S
LIVING
HAND
more merely body. Nonetheless, the Dasein that ‘“‘we’’ are is dispersed
into embodiment, into corporeality. Such dispersion (Zerstreuung) is it-
self grounded in Dasein’s thrownness (Geworfenheit). A certain necessity
accompanies Dasein in its having to be, drawing it into embodiment. Yet
why is Dasein’s Being not primarily corporeality (Leibhaftigkeit)? What is
at first given in Dasein’s environment (Umwelt) is indeed not what is cog-
nized in a theoretical perceiving, but what is to hand, within reach and
grasp (das in Greif- und Reichweite Zuhandene)."' But such Being-to-hand
is itself a founded presence, and this is even more the case for corporeal-
ity, itself in turn founded in Being-to-hand. Any analysis that would claim
a primacy of the corporeal as something simply at hand (Vorhanden)
would itself only be possible as a modification of the more original (yet
still founded) presence
of Being-to-hand. Dasein itself, however, is nei-
ther simply at hand nor to hand. Nevertheless, there belongs to Dasein a
certain kind of “‘Vorhandenheit”’ (in scare quotes, to be sure), a “being at
hand”’ proper (eigen) to it: its facticity, the facticity of its thrownness (SZ,
55—56/82).
Yet what is nearer to us than the body, our bodies, other bodies
that we feel and touch? And yet—feeling, grasping, touching do not in
the first instance explicitly bring us nearer to things. This paper in my
hands is precisely not what is nearest to me; what is nearest to me at this
moment is rather what I am showing, what I myself am held by, what
holds my thoughts, what I am pointing toward. Such pointing and show-
ing, to be sure, will not have taken place without the hand. Or, perhaps,
the hands. What is the hand?
In his essay ““Geschlecht II: Heidegger’s Hand,”’ to which he re-
fers in De l’esprit in insisting that the interpretation of the hand ‘“‘domi-
nates ... Heidegger’s most continuous discourse’ (DE, 28/11),
Derrida examines the role of the hand in Was heift Denken? and in the
Parmenides \ectures. By way of recollection, I shall here give only the
briefest summary of some crucial points from Was heift Denken?
Firstly, the meditation on the hand here occurs in a meditation on
what calls for thinking. Thinking is said to be a, indeed the Hand-werk, the
work of the hand, a designation going back at least to The Origin of the
Work of Art (1935). Just as the origin of the artwork was there said to be
precisely not the artist, but art, so we might surmise that the “‘origin”’ of
the Handwerk of thinking is not the thinker, but the hand.
Secondly, Heidegger claims that the hand cannot be understood
merely as a corporeal organ for grasping. An animal such as the ape has
such organs, but no hand. The hand belongs only to a thinking being.
Thirdly, what is accomplished by the hand?


112
WILL
McNEILL
The hand [says Heidegger] does not merely grasp and catch
[greift und fangt nicht nur]...
. The hand extends and receives
[reicht und empfangt], and not just things, but extends [itself]
and receives [itself] in the other [hand] [reicht sich und empfangt
sich in der anderen]. The hand holds. The hand carries. The
hand draws and signs [Die Hand zeichnet|, presumably because
man is a sign [ein Zeichen].
.
.. Every movement of the hand in
each of its works
.
.
. gestures [gebdrdet sich] within the element
of thinking. (WAD, 51/16)
The movement of the hand is perhaps the very gesture (Gebdrde) of think-
ing. The hand, presumably, can draw and sign (zezchnen) only because it
is itself drawn in and by the gesture of thinking. But what is the draw of
thinking, what is it that draws and calls for thinking? According to the
guiding motif of Was heift Denken?, what calls for thinking is the fact that
we are not yet thinking. This “‘not yet”’ is not some human failing, some-
thing we might remedy. Nor does it point toward something yet to be
achieved in the future. The apparent “‘future”’ of this “not yet’’ has less
to do with something still outstanding than with the “always already,”’
with that which withdraws. What is it that withdraws? Quoting again
from Was heibt Denken?:
What withdraws from us precisely draws us along with it in so
doing, whether or not we immediately take note of this at all.
Whenever we enter the draw of withdrawal [den Zug des
Entziehens], we are drawn [auf dem Zug]—only in quite a differ-
ent way than migratory birds [Zugvdgel]'*—towards what at-
tracts, draws us towards it [wns anzieht] in its withdrawing. If, as
the ones thus drawn [die so Angezogenen], we are drawn towards
what draws us, then our essence is already stamped [gepragt] by
this “drawn towards...” [‘‘awf dem Zuge zu’’] Drawn towards
what withdraws, we ourselves point towards that which is with-
drawing...
.
Insofar as man 7s thus drawn towards, he, as the one thus
drawing, points [zeigt] towards that which withdraws. As the one
thus pointing, man is the one who points and shows [der
Zeigende].
.
.
. Drawn towards what withdraws, man is a sign
[ein Zeichen]. Because however this sign points towards what
withdraws, it points [deutet] not so much towards that which
there with-draws, but rather towards the withdrawing. The sign
remains without interpretation [ohne Deutung]. (WAD, 5-6 /
9-10)


113
SP
RTs
LIVING
HAND
What withdraws, therefore, is nothing determinate. It is nothing, no
thing. It is the withdrawing itself. This withdrawing is not other than the
sign, it is of the sign. The sign itself remains without interpretation be-
cause all interpretation would already be drawn in a particular direction;
it would presuppose a gathering of logos, or of Geist, that could only bea
sign of withdrawal, a sign of an irretrievable thrownness and dispersion
of all interpretation—and of all questioning.
Now Derrida in “‘Geschlecht II” makes much of the claim that
Heidegger “always thinks the hand in the singular,” quoting for example
the statement from the Parmenides lecture course that ‘Man does not
‘have’ hands, but the hand occupies, in order to have in hand, the es-
sence of man” (Der Mensch “hat” nicht Hande, sondern die Hand hat das
Wesen des Menschen inne).'* This claim concerns Derrida’s reading of logos
in Heidegger as gathering or Versammlung. To quote Derrida:
What comes to man through logos or speech (das Wort) can only
be one single hand. Hands, that is already or still the organic
or technical dissipation [dispersion]. So one will not be sur-
prised faced with the absence of all allusion, for example in the
Kantian style, to the play of difference between right and left,
to the mirror or the pair of gloves. This difference cannot be
sensible.
.
.
. the sole sentence in which Heidegger, to my
knowledge, names man’s hands in the plural seems to concern
precisely the moment of prayer, or in any case the gesture in
which the two hands join together [sich falten] to make them-
selves only one in simplicity [Einfalt]. Gathering together [Ver-
sammlung] is always what Heidegger privileges. On the other
hand, nothing is ever said of the caress or of desire. Does one
make love, does man make love, with the hand or with the
hands? (GIJ, 182)'*
I find Derrida’s claim here somewhat worrying, because it gives the im-
pression that he is thinking Versammlung in straightforward opposition
to dispersion. Yet the gathering of Versammlung, whether we translate it as
logos, Geist, or the Greek hen (the One),'° has never been understood in
this way by Heidegger. For Heidegger, Versammlung does not mean the
opposite of dispersion; it does not mean the single as opposed to the
multiple. Rather, it is always thought as a belonging together involving a
withdrawal that cannot be understood in terms of unity or oneness, as
the Er-eignis/Ent-eignis of identity and difference.
Derrida, moreover, is inaccurate in some of his comments here.
_Not only do we find a discussion, in section 23 of Sein und Zeit, of Da-


WILL
McNEILL
sein’s becoming spatialized into ‘‘corporeality”’ (leiblichkeit), a discussion
dealing precisely with the problem of Dasein’s hands, left and right, and
indeed in the context of the Kantian approach to the phenomenon of
orientation; even more significant is the way this analysis is itself prefig-
ured in the 1925 course to which I shall now revert.
In section 25 of the course, Heidegger insists that the possibility
of showing (Zeigen) pertaining to signs in our surrounding environ-
ment—to signs, that is, that have already been dispersed—is grounded
in the phenomenon of orientation:
Dasein is oriented as corporeal, as corporeal it is in each case
itself its right and left, and therefore parts of the body [die
Leibteile] are also right and left. This means: it belongs to the
Being of corporeal things [Leibdinge] that they are also consti-
tuted by orientation. There is no hand in general [Es gibt keine
Hand viberhaupt], rather every hand and every glove is right or
left, because in its usage the glove in accordance with its sense
is designed to go along with the bodily movements [die Leib-
bewegungen]. (GA 20, 319-20/232—emphasis added)
There is no hand in general. Dasein as corporeal, as dispersed into embodi-
ment, is already orientated. Its hands are already orientated in accord-
ance with right or left. Yet left and right can never be understood simply
in terms of one another. Rather, such orientation depends upon a direc-
tionality (Ausgerichtetheit) of Dasein itself. And this directionality presup-
poses a certain horizonal gathering of Dasein that would be involved in
all factical dispersion.'® Thus involved, it would not simply be prior to
such dispersion.
Relating this to the later context of Was heift Denken?, we may say
that if the hand—the singular hand—is that which originally points,
shows, signs, designates, and draws us into a particular direction of
thinking, then this hand is not the embodied hand as merely at hand
(vorhanden). It is not the hand as inscribed. Yet neither is it to be under-
stood as the hand in general, abstracted from all embodiment. The sin-
gularity of the hand, the singularity of its draw, is not that of an
individuated hand, nor that of a unitary essence of the hand. The gather-
ing of the hand is neither regulated nor prescriptive, for this hand has
already withdrawn in being gathered and drawn toward that which
would attempt to regulate.
What, then, of the hand that would write of spirit, de l’esprit? Hav-
ing looked, albeit fleetingly, at the way in which De l’esprit begins, I shall
conclude with some remarks on its ending.


115
SPIRIT'S
LIVING
HAND
Is De lesprit itself haunted by the very accusation it levies against
Heidegger with respect to his Trakl reading, namely that the gestures he
employs are sometimes simply “caricatural’’? (DE, 178/108). Does Der-
rida merely deconstruct a caricature of Heidegger and his Geist? Such
remains my suspicion. Could it be otherwise? Could any deconstruction,
or indeed Destruktion, begin other than by taking as its point of departure
certain formal presuppositions—presuppositions which, in their very
givenness, would have to remain unquestioned, because they would al-
ready have drawn the questioner into beginning?
Toward the conclusion of De l’esprit, Derrida engages in a retreat
(retraite). He retreats in acknowledging the problem of the trait or draw
(le trait, der Zug), this time with respect to Heidegger’s Trakl interpreta-
tion. He concedes that the difference or duality within the draw of Geist
is not that of division, that Geist as the One of Versammlung does not
mean identity, indifference or uniformity, but “the most matutinal
morning”’ of what is “‘quite other.’’ He retreats with the words: “It is too
late, and I will not detain [or hold: retiendra] you until the morning”’ (DE,
176/107).
Derrida will not detain us, because this morning remains to come
(reste ad venir). As what is “‘earlier’’ than “‘the early” (die Frithe), it will
never arrive.!’ At least not in the text. Derrida retreats, as he continues,
“by schematizing to the extreme.”’ But what is significant about this re-
treat is that he retreats, withdraws from the scene, by making Heidegger
retreat, by schematizing an imaginary conversation. He retreats
by ventril-
oquizing Heidegger. In “‘this retreat [retraite] of Heidegger’s” he “recon-
structs’ and has Heidegger withdraw some of the very “‘strategies’’—as
he calls them—by which he, Derrida, organized his reading of Heideg-
ger: He has Heidegger deny that he is simply “opposed”’ to anything;
deny that spirit as flame is “‘something other” (autre chose) than the tradi-
tional pneumatological conception; deny, finally, having said that Gezst is
“‘at first’ or in the first instance (d’abord) this or that (DE, 182/111-12).
De esprit closes, stages its final withdrawal, with Derrida having Heideg-
ger’s interlocutors insist on the necessity of maintaining, of not interrupt-
ing the dialogue between Heidegger and the poet, between Heidegger
and themselves. Then, suddenly, this imagined conversation is itself in-
terrupted—interrupted as the interlocutors acknowledge the return of
something unavoidable: “Spirit which keeps watch in returning [en reve-
nant] will always do the rest. Through the flame or the ashes, but as the
entirely other, unavoidable [le tout autre, inévitablement].”” Anticipating
the “‘irruptive” return of this entirely other, Derrida writes that any at-
tempt to say this entirely other would consign the words of the tradition


116
WILL
McNEILL
(thus of the history of ontology) to being “‘paleonyms”’: ““They require
only the necessary quotation marks to suspend them in a writing or read-
ing which must [or is to: dott] carry us beyond” (DE, 177/108). A little
later, on the next page, he lists in quotation marks some of the terms
which would be unsettled by such an irruption, adding in parentheses: “‘I
place quotation marks around so many obscure words.”’
If the quotation marks gather round toward the end of De l’esprit,
crowding in and imposing themselves on the very hand that would write
their law, this return is undoubtedly that of a singular spirit, heteroge-
neous to the origin “‘of all the laws and assignments which are our very
memory” (DE, 176/107). The singularity of this Geist is not that of a
regulation or a law, nor does it have need of quotation marks for its
inscription. Its singularity—is it not that of the living hand, of the hand
that extends, reaches, touches, and writes? Like the hand of the poet
John Keats, which penned the fragment:
This living hand, now warm and capable
Of earnest grasping, would, if it were cold
And in the icy silence of the tomb,
So haunt thy days and chill thy dreaming nights
That thou wouldst wish thine own heart dry of blood
So in my veins red life might stream again,
And thou be conscience-calmed. See here it is—
I hold it towards you.
